It may be of interest to you to know the follow-

SIR.

log : On the 2nd October last, I purchased 13 Hens and a Cook—all cross-bred—and a very miserable lot they looked; in fact, they hadbeen half famished. I gave them hot food in the morning—boiled potatoes and meal, with dripi,ing, bacon liquor, or such like stuff in it—and every other day about a teaspoonful of OVUM well mixed up. Result: up to present date, 2nd January, 513 eggs. Not a bad record, eh ? especially seeing that four were old Hens. OVUM is GRAND Stuff. Yours truly. WM. COEFIBLD. "0 V U 11. "

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAEEST NELLIE,

I It seems that we are again to ne permitted the Ichoice between cs.pe or coat for the cowing see.- , eon; or, better still, we may indulge in both, and so enjoy a pleasing variety. Certainly with some dresses a cape has a better effect than a jacket, awl vice versa. A " dressy " casings is of black velvet, with two frills of moirsi oak roeuai the shoulders, outinang a yoke striped with narrow jet, while a sequin drop edging trim the frills. The lining is surah, and it is certainly a oheap little cape. Another cape has mach style with its flounced sleeves of velvet, the back and the batque being of moire, while a rudhe of ribbon gives n pretty finiTh to the tight-fitting front.

I, The gargon eafo vest Ls in sealskin or musquah fur, with large revers extending far below the waist, and meant to be either left open or olosed. A band of the same fur coltdeee the doable puffed sleeves to the el-sow; small ribbon bows divide the velvet puffing from the cloth one. Cat's head tcque, fashioned with two rosette bows in black velvet, displaying in owl centre an oval buckle in pasta diamonds and emeralds. Ear-shaped loops in satin ribbon are intermingled with white and -black ospreys. Tunics and overskirts are certainly becoming fashionable, and I quite anticipate the reign of draped skirts next SOLIOU. Tasks of moos:dime pleated gauze or not are shown by one or two leading houses, and the pointed apron of sequin or spangled net over a silk or satin gown is an accom- plished fact. The long pelisse or coat is much- patronised in very handsome materials, but the shape is copied in fine cloth and vigogne, or in broad diagonal serge, with trimmings of velvet and fur. A very effective driving or travelling cloak is in water- cress-green cloth, black velvet, and dark brown fur; or it is very useful la all brown, the cloth being of a cinnamon shade, the velvet quite a deep brown. The back is well shaped, the fronts semi- fitting and having a straight appearance; or brown plush may be used for the shoulder-cape, and can be edged with fur, and no fur used down the fronts. If sufficiently thick cloth is employed, no lining is required, and this simplifies the make and lessens the expense. The fronts can atvrays be faced with a half-width of silk, and the sleeves lined with Italian cloth or inexpensive silk. Five yards of cloth will be required for this wrep and about two yards of plush. The muff can be made from the old pieees, und should be lined with three or four layers of wadding.

Boiled Bread Pudding.

Cut some stale bread into rather thin slices, butter'a pudding basin, and lay a piece of the bread well buttered at the bottera of the basin, then spread some jam (rasp- barry or black currant) on the bread, then more bread buttered and jam, until the basin is three-parts full, make a custard with a pint of milk and three %Owl* eggs aryll beaten, pour into the basin, tie a cloth over the top, and boil three-quarters of an hour. Turn out carefully on to a hot disband serve at once.

"lon are rig's!. in your fancy," reeponded Lord Adlowe, with a complacent expreesion of counten- ance. "The Li.ply Beatrice has promisod to con- sider my odor, and to give me an answer wheat I shall ask for it." The earl was surprised and delighted. "Why, that i positive enoonragemont of your suit!" tau exclaimed.

" Ost-tainly said Lord Adlmre, quietly. "The Lady lieetrice meant it as such. My long waiting has not beon without effect. I look forward my dear frond, to a speedy union with your daughter." "I scarcely }mow what to say, I ant so aston- ished," declared Lord Hampton, in complete bewil- derment. " Before you came in this evening I spoke to Beatrice about you, and -she declared, as she heel cloud a thousand times before, that she uhould never marry. Only last weeks he refused tiae Duke of Lamdford, one of t-iwe best matches in And sow aho encourages you to believe that she will become your wife?' 49413 seeyi strange WO I should succeed whore ao int-iY have fatted,''' asaeskod the guest, with a siatiafi.3d look. "1 mast rojoioe at my good fortune without enquiring into its cause too closely. 14 may be pique on the part of the Lady Bea- trice. I scarcely dare flatter inystelf that it is love."

The earl smiled again involuntarily. The idea that the Lady Beatrice Ifampton--so icy, so statue- like—could love anyone, seemed preposterous. "'or the present, of course, we must keep the faot to ourselves that the Lady Beatrice has deigned to smile upon my suit," remarked Lord Adlowe, putting aside his empty cup. "That she has taken my proposals into consideration, my dear Earl, makes it necessary for me to declare to you my prospects, which 1. will de frankly." "It is not necessary:" said the earl. "I have known you from your boyhood, Adlowe, and am more than satisfled with the possibilities of a near relationship between us. I know of no one whom I would so gladly welcome as a son-in-law." " Thanks ; but the explanation must be made, nevertheless. First, lam the inheritor of the title and debts of my late father ; " and Lord Adlowe's tone was decidedly ironical. "I have been some- what dissipated, as report has it, and travel and too great profuseness of expenditure have somewhat cramped my resources. That is one side of the picture. Contrast against it the fact lam the de- clared heir of my Uncle Trevelyan, my mother's brother, and there is a handsome offset to my shortcomings. lam a great favourite with Lord Trevelyan, who, harsh and eccentric as he is, miserly as are his habits is always generous to me."

"Lord Trevelyan has the fertuse of a prince, and since his brother's son, Geoffrey Trevelyan, ie dead, you are his legal heir," repliod the earl. "When you come into the Trevelyan estates, you will be one of the richest noblemen in the kingdom, Adlowe. With your uncle's wealth united to Beatrice's fortune, you will have a colossal income. I 'twilit like to see the two fortunes wedded. "And so should 1," said Lord Adlowe, with a significance that escaped the notice of his host. " Where in all England could be found a lady so suitod to. dispense that wealth with grace and dignity as the Lady Beatrice? I lore her, my lord, and I shall have no object in life save to win bor."

"My influence shall be used in your favour," declared the earl, his pride and ambition quicken- ing as he thought of the brilliant prize within reach of his daughter. "I am getting old and want to see Beatrice settled beforel die. You are the first person she ever permitted to hope for her favour since Geoffrey Trevelyan died, and I believe that she will yet become your wife." Lord Adlo we's eyes sparkled, and his face beamed with hopeful resolution. He knew well enough that Lady Beatrice detested him and that he could never win her save through her fears. He saw that he had in some inexplicable way touched upon those fours in his recent interview, else she would have gives him a haughty and curt dismissal. He knew, too, that unless he could obtain some hold upon her she would never marry him. To solve the mystery surrounding her was now his object. He had the mystery in his thoughts an he said, carelessly : " Will not the Lady Beatrice return to us this evening, my lord?" . . .

" think not," stammered the earl, his face flushing. "Beatrice has retired to her room, and she never likes to be distil-bed when she seeks •seclusion."

"But I understood that she was to return."

_ "Oh, that makes a difference," said Lord Hamp- ton; his, brow clearing. "I will ,send a message— no, I'll go myself and request her presence. Excuse me a moment, AdLowe.'

He arose and departed OD his errand. Lord Adlowe stole after him to the door, lis- tened, and then crept half-way up the stairs, from which point he could hear the proceedings of the: earl.

Lord Hampton hastened along the upper hall to a suite of rooms immediately ever the drawing- room. He knocked upon the principal door, first softly, then more loudly. No one answered him. He turned the know, but the door was locked. He called his daughter's name • softly, yet in a penetrating voice. There was no response. There were other doors along the hall, opening into the different chambers comprised in ,the suite of the Lady Beatrice, and at each one of these the earl knocked softly calling upon his daughter's name. Still there came back no reply. There was no sound of life within those chambers. The Lady Beatrice might be lying dead within, or senseless, for all the evidence to the contrary. The earl's face was convulsed with agitation. " What is this mystery ?" he whispered, leaning against the door. "Is Beatrice wrapped in the thrall of some delirium-producing drug or liquor? Is she awake? What is she doing ? , My God I what a puzzle she is to me ! Only a few inohei§ of wall or door between us, and I am puzzling my' brain and agonizing my soul with suspicions of what may be on the other side! This is terrible—un- bearable ! " Again he listened for a token of movement with- in the rooms. They were as soundless as a tomb. "There is no use in lingering here," he thought. "Beatrice will not make her appearance again to- night. It is always so. When she shuts herself up in this manit'6r she remains secluded ft- hours. Let her guard her secret. I have no longer a wish to fathom it. Perhaps lam happier in tpy igno ' ranee than I should he in a full knowledge of it." He sighed so .heavily that the inspiration was almost a moan. Then he moved from the door towards the stair- . • ease. Lord Adlowe noiselessly flitted on before him, entering the drawing-room without having been seen. 4 The earl descended the stairs, and paused in the 'Cower hall to command his features and to repress , his agitation. He fancied he had succeeded, when heiopened the door and entered the presence of his guest; and certainly Adlowe's unconcerned manner went far to confirm the opinion. "Beatrice regrets that she cannot join us again this evening," said Lord Hampton' with an appear- ance of sincerity. •• "The dear girl was agitated by her recent interview with you, and having a head- ache—"

"No apologies are necessary," interrupted Lord Adlowe, as the earl instinctively hesitated in his sppech. "rtihould be sorry to disturb her lady- ship,under the circumstance& ' I will call Upon ler tomorrow, or after ray return from the Country." Ale earl sighed wearily. It was hard for him, with his clear perceptions of integrity and honour, to offer that false explanations, but he accepted this course as the only one left him. Before all other considerations came the incvitab:e necessity of screening his daughter.

" Boatrioe will net be Yielble before three to- morrow," he said. "Thee.. town dissipations tell upon her. lau going to take her into the country as soon as the warm weather oomee—say in May. We shall be your neighbours then, Adlowe, for Tro- yelyan Park Js not many mile. distant from our country home. You will stop at the Park, I sup- po3?"

Lord Adlowe repliod in the afnrmatiye. The earl roemned hisaeat, and tile two pursued their conversation with an appearanoe of mterest in it; hut.all the while the father's heart wandered to the mystory of his daughter's grange seclusion, and all the while Adlowe studied upon the same subject.

The two gentlemen were in the midst of an ap- psrently aba.rbing discussion e,oncerning a porson lor whom neither cared, when a livoried porter, whose duty it vras to stand Dear the entrance door, hurriedly bnrs-t into the drawing-room, his face the pierure of wildest alarm.

The earl leaped to his feet. "The room on firs ! " he ejaculated. "Yoe, my lord. I'll give the alarm." "Not for your life!' commanded his master, iternly. "Say nothing to your fellow-seryants. Stay in the lower hall."

His lordship pushed aside the astounded ser- vitor, dashed up the stairs, three steps at a time, and rushed to his daughter's roems. Lord Adlowo followed at his heels. There was smoke in the hall, issuing from one of the rooms of the Lady Beatrice. Clearly somethint was lisurning within. A group of servants, anxions and frightened, ,tood near the door. A HISTORIC TREE.

A friend, recently returned from a visit to the old country, in telling me of the different things of interest he had seen, remarked,—" You may think it strange that I, who was never a rabid anti- slavery man, should have gone almost twenty miles out of my way for the sole purpose of sitting under the old oak tree where Wilberforce sat, with Pitt and Granville, when he first made known his in tention to bring the subject of the Aloolition of the Slave Trade before Parliament."

The tree in question is a venerable oak, on the grounds of Mr. Pitt's estate of Holwood, in the parish of Keston, five miles south from Bromley. And here it was, on the greensward, beneath the shadow of this historic tree, that the great phil- anthropist sounded the first note of that grand onset which was, in the end, to wipe out the African Slave-Trade ! Wilberforce himself says: "I got together at my house, from time to time, persons who knew anything about the matter; and when I had acquired all the information I felt that I required, I called Pitt and Grenville to my assist- ance. We met under the old oak, where I laid out my plans. I wanted Pitt to go on with it; bat he said, no. He said I was the one to under- take its conduct as a subject suited to my character told *lents."

Earl Stanhope has recently comnsemorated this meeting, with excellent taste, by causing to be erected upon the spot a seat, on the back of which is the following inscription: "From Mr. Wilbellorce's Diary, 1788. "At length, I well remember, after a conversa- tion with Mr. Pitt, in the open ait, at the root of an old tree at Holwood, just above the steep de- scent into the vale of Kestou, I resolved to give notice, on a fitting occasion, in the House of Com- mons, of my intention to bring forward the aboli- tion of the Slave Trade." _ _ _

. My friend said that the old tree, which is ex- ceedingly gnarled, with its root's, in places, pro- jecting above ground, is preserved with religious care, and is known throughout the kingdom as " Wilberforce's Oak."

The story of Wilberforce's noble efforts before the House are too well-known to require com- ment. C.

Stewed Ox Kidney.

Skin an ox kidaey, and cut it in -rather thick rounds, melt a little dripping in a frying pan, fry the kidney a nice brown, and, as it is fried, put it in a nice clean saucepan. While you fry the vegetables peel and cut in slices two large onions, two carrots, two turnips, and two potatoea. Fry all these vegetables a nice brown, put them into the saucepan with the kidney, just cover them with boiling water, let simmer three-quarters of an hour. Put a tablespoonful of flour in a basin, mix smooth with a little cold water, pour this into the kid- ney stew, let it boil two n.iinutes, turn into a hot vege- table dish, sprinkle over a little chopped parsley, pepper and salt, and serve as hot as possible. •

THE POSITION OF LANCASHIRE MINERS.

Mr. S. Woods, M.P., in his seventeenth annual report to the miners in a district extending from ..s7;.t. Helens to .Bolton, of which he is the agent, said he trusted as the years rolled on they were gaining a broader and richer experience in the great and important industries and labour problems, which were not only of the hjgliest importance t) the miners, but which were at the present time engaging the serious and deliberate attention of

• - statesmen as well as of every clasS of the community. The great labour movements of the country, of which their movement formed a part, were still pressing thei r claims and making progress. The great and unpreced- ented lockout in the coal trade in the month of July, 1893, would be an event in therhistory of the country, if not of the world. The demand made by the colliery, owners of 25 per cent. reduction from the miners' wages was not only an error in tactics, but unreasonable, uzijust, and unwarranted. It was alleged, and probably with truth, that the object of the'aftack was. threefold. (1) To force a substantial reduction, in order to further stimulate local and ruinous competition ; (2) to get rid ot surplus stocks of coal at fabulous prices ; and (3) to break the back (and con sectuently to destroy the influ- ence) of the miners' organisation. In the lockout there were two points that should •• not .be overlooked—viz.„ the inestimable services rendered by Lord Rosehery on the settlement of the dispute and the unprecedented, lavish, and spontaneous generosity of the public of the country towards the miners and their fats ilies, both of which merited the undying gratitute of the whole of the toilers of Great Britain. During the year, notwith- standing the extraordinary experience through which they had passed, it was satisfaatory to find, especially in the face of prophecies to the contrary, that their mem - bership and finances ha l not ouly been maintained, but show4d hopeful signs of _increase. The number of mem- bers reniained practically steady at about 13,000, some- times a few us Ire and sometimes a few less, being the result of transfers backward,s. and forwards, hut he thought they might assume that both numerically 'and financially their condition was Satisfactory. That, hs regretted, was net the case in every district in the county, and looking at the general state of the organisa- tion in the whole of the country he thought it was highly unsatisfactory in some of the districts. No doubt they would be fully aware that steos were now being, taken to reorganise Lancashire pratty rouch on the same prin- ciple as Yorkshire, and ho hoped that no personal feelings or local prejudices wpuld stand in the way of the new scheme, when revised, being adopted as the general basis of consolidation.

THE AMAZONS.

Thirty years ago Sir Richard Burton, then Captain Burton, was sent by I..e,rd Russell on a Mission te Dahomey with presents feom the Queen. On his return he told his wife that he had seen enough dreadful sights to turn his brain. At that time, says tho " St. James's Gazette," there were no Europeans at Dahomey ; and the savagery of the King and his people amply accounted for the fact. Burton's story of the Mission is full of curious things, and his widow has done well to republish the work, which would ctherwise ha difficult of access. Perhaps the most interesting part of it is that which relates to the so-called Amazons, "women crueller and fiercer than woo."

Burton explains their oriain as troops by saying that the State of Dahomey included many more women than men, and that military duties were therefore not unsuited to the superfluous ladies. He adds !—"At tho Dahomey Court, curious to say in Africa, women take precedence of uten ; yet, with truly Hamitic contradic- tiousnses, the warrioresses say, ' We are no longer females, but males ' ; and a soldier disgracing himself is called, in insult, a woman. It is clear, therefore, that they owe their dignity to the fiction of being rcyal wives. Wherever a she-soldiery is, celibacy must been. of its rules."

Burton's account of those ladies is very graphic. H. says, speaking of the year 1863 :—" I saw all thee*, women trosps marching on service out of Yana. The officers, distinguished by their white head-cloths alai by an esquirtss-at-arms, gonerally a small.s!ave-girl carry- ing the musket, led their commands. I expected to sea Penthesileas, Thalestrises, Dianas—lovely names ! I saw old, ugly, square-built frows, trudging 'grumpily' along, with the face of ' cook' after being much 'nagged 'by the noissus.' The privates carried pac ks on cradles like those of the male soldiery, containing their bed-mats, alothes, and food for a week or a fort- night, mostly toasted grains and bean-cake hot with peppers. Cartridge pouches of two different shapes were girt round their waists, and slung to their sides were water-goerds, fetish sacks, bullet-wallets, powder cala- bashes, fails (sic), little cutlasses, wooded pipe-cases enveloped sin leather tobacco bags, flint, steel, and tinder and Lilliputian stools, with three or four legs, cut out of single blocks. Their weapons were slung, and behind their backs dangled their hats, scarecrow felts, eatin- guishers of white cotton useful as sacs de nuit,umbrellas of plaited palm-leaf, and low-crowned, broad-brimmed home-made straw hats."

ICompared with this force In "heavy marching order" we will give Burton's account of another detachment in " review order" :—" It was now the Amazon's turn to advance, and they came up in better style as regards marching and firing than their brother soldiers. They passed us witia a ' feu d'enfer,' and when the dust was not flying the smoke hung like a pall upon the ground. . . . Those soldiers wore tunics of grey bat S, stainod brown with blood and barks, covering the bosom and extending to the knees like men, short drawers and white sashes hanging to the right." The same Amazon warriors at the annual games, or "King's Customs," which include a great slaughter ef criminals and prisoners, are thus described :—" Fourteen of the tallest and most swaggering, not including a small girl, formed line, and two mistresses of the ceremonies armed with horse-tail chaurie, chanted and sometimes 'pranced to give time in rear. The toilette was the pink of propriety—a vest, I pagne, a shorter uoclergarb, and Ffon ehokcto or pantilettero' longer than the. male article, and extending to the knees. When the time was t9obe changed, one or both of the Blue Companies [picked troops] advanceu towards the band with pin toes straight in front, ungracefully throning out first one arm, then the other, with.one or two fingers extended, and clap- ping palms on the thighs. Thus Use measure was taken up by a single cymbal and finally by the full band. The dancers stamped, wriggled, kicked the dust with one foot, sing, shuffled, wrung their hands—there is over a suspicion of beta acting in these performances—bending almost double, ducking heads, and ending in a prestis- simo and very violent movement of the shoulders, hips, and loins. Then, while the rest reposed on short stools,. there was a grand pas do deux. The whole merits of the ballet were time and unison. Nothing could be less graceful or more deficient in the poetry of motion. The recent French campaign in Dahomey and the sue- cesE•es of General Dodds have drawn a great deal of at- tention to these fighting ladies, who seem to be carefully distinguished from the " ee facto" wives of the King. Sir R. Burton estima.tes their numbers thus, and it was just before a vigorous campaign in 1864, in which the flower of the Amazon airily lost their lives :—" Before 10 a.m. were counted 1,439 mostly wea ported; they then marched in knots, in all 246, making, when wei retired to breakfast, a total of 1,685. The movement was in- terrupted till nor return alien the Bing set out with a body-guard of 353. Thus the grand total was 2,038, and at most, allowing for omissions, 2,500. But of these one- thild were unarmed, or half-armed, leaving the fighting women at a figure of 1,700. . . . Commander Forbes and Mr. Beecroft (1849-50) give 5,000 ; but the heroines, like Vie commissariA cittle in Afghanistan, were marched out of one gate and in at another. . . . The fact is, these most illustrious viragoes are now a mere handful." •

The King's body-guard above-mentioned are elsewhere described as "young women in high training." At one of their reviews Bir IL Burton tells us that the King made his inspection in a Bath chair—a recent present from a "committee of English philanthropes," and that at these reviews the Amazons and the male troops are car/dully Separated by a line of bamboos, which is never transgressed. When on the march, or returning to quarters, the roads are cleared of ail passengers, who - must wait with averted lace until " the illustrious vira- goes" have passed by.

" STIOr-LIFTIN G " BY WOMEN.

COMBINATION OF LONDON 'TRADESMEN.

"Shop-lifting "by women has become so rife that many tradesmen in London have bound themselves to prosecutd in every detected case. At the North London Police Ccurt two " ladies "—so called because they wer& expensively dressed—were charged with several thefts of small articles from grocers' shops. They admitted having taken the articles, but their advocate pleaded in &fence, first, that they were in good positions ; secondly, that one of them was subject to alternate fits of excitement and depression ; and thirdly, that the whole affair was a practical joke. Mr. Lane, the magis- trate, refused to accept any of these somewhat conflict- ing explanations, and remanded the "ladies " to prison. until Friday.

The third great demonstration of the Austrian Socialists was held on Sunday in Vienna, the spot selected being the greworks meadow of the Prater. It was 'attended by about 30,000 't)eople. Speeches demanding universal suffrage were made, Herr Schubmeier and Fraulein Dworjak being the principal speakers. TI-e most perfect order was maintained, both at the meeting and subsequently in the streets of the capital. Few policemen were to be seen.

MAXIMS WORTH REMEMBERING.

Don't live beyond your income. Don't talk home affairs in public. Don't have too many particular friends. Don't question apparent eroool motives in others. • DoD't expect perfection in this world, or yours will be a life of bitter disappcintnaent. ' Don't worry over the past which cannot be recalled, nor the future you may never see, thus embitter- ing the present which is all we have. Don't force your cpinions or prejudices in opposition to the actual experience of others. If you are too bigoted to be convinced have the gentility to con- ceal it. Don't criticise or undervalue the testimony of • your countrymen as to the value of Warner's Safe Cure, • for their statements are made in good faith ; actuated by gratitude and founded on sober experience. "I have for years' since I left India." writes Rev. Wilson Haff,:ulden, of Church House, Bor- desley Green, Birmingliarni " been a perfect. martyr to indigestion caused by sluggish liver, and ha va tried various doctors and their medi- cines without any permanent benefit. I was in- duced to try Warner's Safe Cure and rills, with the result that I am now completely restored to health." Don't neglect Nature's warnings and wait until serious diseases develop.

'CAPITAL" CHAT.

Great anxiety is being shown by the Committee of the London and North-Western Insurance Society, to remove the effect of their sweeping defeats at the two open meetings of North-Western Railway ser- vants in London. I hear that the Wolverton meet- ing, reported a day or two ago, was rigidly confined by ticket. I also hear that at other North-Western centres a strong feeling has set in against the policy *f the Committee, to which is attrinuted the loss of the greatly extended protection given to all who work in dangerous trades by .Mr. .Asquith's bill. Many North-Western men in the last few days have intimated that they cannot and will not stand alone against the movement of labour reform. They have wished, and still wish, to maintain their in- surance fund, but they repudiate the role, thrnst. upon them, of defending universal .contracting out. What their ballot meant was that they wanted to keep the insurance, not that they wanted to con, tract oat; and more and more of them probably are recognising, since the last debates that there is no logical connection between insurance and contract- ing out. This shifting from the attitude to which tile persuasions of the Committee bad led, the majority will be strengthened by the meetings now to be organised to demand a thoroughgoing bill.

The rumours as to Mr. Gladstone's impending resignation, which seemed to have received their .quietus,were revived again on Monday. They took' indeed, a somewhat circumstantial form even in the lobby, but it wits easy to trace their origin eastwards to the City. It is indicative of the keen interest which is felt in regard to the Prime Minister's position that even a cursory ob- servation which he is reported to have let fall in. replying to the Scorch deputation on Monday, to the efbet that Scotch Home Rale would not be .a question to be dealt with in his day, caused a cer-, tam amount of sensation. As a matter of fact, it was only a repetition of a remari; Which the Prime Minister had made more than once without awaken- ing surprise or even attracting attention. It is obvious that Scotch Home Rule belongs necessarily to a somewhat distant future. However, there can be no concealment of the fact that the alarming' rumours which have been persistently set on root and ,the somewhat guarded denials which have been forthcoming have a little unsettled men's minds if not as to the immediate—for no one be- lieves in the story as to immediate resignation—at, all events as to the approximate future. It ,is cer- tain at all events that, whether any justification exists or not,more attention is now paid to these re-- ports in usually Well informed quarters than at any previous time.

The:National Union of Teachers, which is regarded by the Education Department as entirely represen- tative of the general body of teachers in public elementary schools, is likely to ditcass at its•confer- ence to be held at Oxford during Easter Week the advisableness of acting somewbat on strict trade- union lines. A debate by the Executive of this body on a proposal to fix a "fair rate of vv ages" for teachers and to agitate for the adoption of such a rate showed a strong feeling in tavour of that course; and although the proposal was met by all amendment which was carried, tile result practi- cally leaves it open for the conference to decide very much in the way originally soggestA.. A plan of _raising a voluntary fund for the augmentation of the wretched salaries paid to thousands of certifi- cated teachers is also under consideration. The ,question of rate aid or other increased subventions to 'Voluntary schools will also arise during the de- liberations of the conference, which is likely to be one of the mcst important ever held by the National 'Union of Teachers,

I have already reported the fact that the "moder- ate" party among the Anarchists, • the men who re- fuse to give-any countenance to a wurder propag- anda, have resolved on an agitation, I bear that their plans now have been completed. While one purpose of the agitation is to persuade us that an- archy and a murder propaganda are not synony- mous, the other is to defeat the aim of those who would make English Anarchy a conspiracy against public safety. The scheme of propaganda includes the establishment of branches in all the great in- dustrial centres.; and the promoters of the move- ment believe that they will soon attract to them- selves all that is best in the Anarchist movement. Spite of the feeling which at present exists towards Anarchists and Anarchy, they propose to commence operations at once,

The Empress Frederick, says a London corres- pondent, is increasingly stout, and gets more and more like the Queen. She has left off the atro- ciously ugly peaked .widow bonnet formerly affect- ed, and now wears an ordinary Queen's widow bonnet, which is mach more becoming. The Em- press is by far the most gifted of the present royal family. Her fight with Bismarck some years ago showed that she possesses great ability, energy,and unusual firmness of character. The Empress ap- peared in public in Loudon on Saturday for the first time during a year.

Last year the Unionists in Ireland systematically boycotted Lord Houghton's levees and receptions in Dublin Castle. The attendance at the opening ceremonies for the season this year. though not so large as when Tory Lords-Lieutenant ruled at the Castle, warranted the belief that the ukase had been withdrawn, and that Irish " society" had determined to enjoy itself in the familiar fashion. But the extremists among the "loyal "party quickly reasserted their power. The news that some lords and ladies had appeared at the board of the Queen's representative spread consternation throughout the ranks of the particularly loyal section of Her Majesty's subjects whose mission is to maintain their ascendency. And their erring brethren were prom;"tly brouet to see the error of their ways.

The word was sent forth that. such dangerous Coquetting with the enemy at the Castle was dropped to be forthwith, and last year's boycott was to be enforced as vigorously as ever. To their disgust the poor butterflie;, of fashion who had pro- raised themselves a merry season at the viceregal Court were compelled to relinquish the idea in Obedience to the inexorable behests of their political leaders. I can sympathise with the victims to some extent. Lord Boughton is a charming host, and he !las shown himself capable of maintaining suah 'Qcspitable traditions as are attached to his office in a far better fashion than his immediate predeces- sors. The loss is all on the side of the boycotters in this instance, as I learn that the Lord-Lieutenant IS more amused than annoyed at the senile conduct of the loyal folk. Notwithstanding the absence of 13, early all the Unionists, except those whose duties bring them to the Castle, last week's fetes were Cheerful enough. .

PRESENTATION AT SANKEY BRIDGES WESLEYAN

pn.M,7"%14.17,

On Saturday evening, the teachers and senior scholars of the Sankey Bridges Sunday School, had a pleasant gathering called together, for the purpose of presenting to Mr. James L. George,a travelling bag, on,the occasion of his resigning his post of superintendeut of the Sunday school, which post he has held since the formation of the school in 1877. Before the meeting was held, about 60 sat down to a substantial tea, and at the meeting the chair was occupied by Mr. J. Summersgill. The Chairman said that it gave him great pleasure to preside at such a meeting as they had that night. He was sure that Mr. George deserved the presentation which was to be made to him, for he had worked hard and well for the school since its foundation. Seventeen years was a long time in a man's history to be associated with a place of worship, and Mr. George deserved credit for the work he had done during that . period. During the time that he (the chairman) was his colleague, he had always found him one easy to work with, and always easy to co-operate with, and his family also had joined with him in the work of the Sunday school. Mr. Summersgill went on to give an interesting and humor- OM history of the school from its foundation, and des- cribed it as equal in interest to almost any place he knew of. Mr. Brough, in making the presentation, said that he was glad to seethat a change was eorning over the coun- trv„for whereas honour used to be given to those who were prominent in the slaughter of their fellowmen, people were beginning to honour greatness of character and self-sacrifice. He took it that that was the reasen of meeting that night. They were doing honour to whom honour was due. For many years, Mr. George and his .family had been a blessing to Sankey Bridges and the church. and to the school with which they were con- nected. (Hear, hear.) He had known him for many years as a colleague in the superintendency of the school, and a kinder hearted man he had never known in his life. He was not saying too much when he said that Mr. George was a Christian indeed. (Hear, hear.) It was with feelings of regret that the teachers accepted his resignation as the morning superintendent, and they felt that they would not like so many years of faithful service to pass over without being, recognised. The hag had been subscribed for by a large number of children as well as by the teachers, which showed in what esteem Mr. George was held. The beg bore the inscription : " Presented to Mr. James Lewis George, by the teachers and scholars of the Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Sandey school, as a token of respect for 17 years faithful ser- vice, Dec. 31st. 1893." He asked Mr. George to accept 'the present in the name of the teachers and scholars of the Sunday school, and he hoped that he would long have health and strength to make use of it. Mr. George, who was warmly received, said that he did not know how to begin to express his 'gratitude to them.. He did not feel worthy of such a beautiful pre- sent. He had never expected such a recognition, and he never felt himself so humble, and at the same time so proud as he did that moment. He had been connected with Sunday schools ever since he was a boy, and had been brought up a Methodist, and he owed almost all his learning to the Sunday school. He went on to give personal reminiscunes, and concluded by again thank- ing them for their kindness to him. Mr. Blythe said that this was a most interesting gathering, and Mr. George well deserved the presenta- tion. He might mention that at the opening of the school there were 20 teachers and 31 scholars, so that it had grown enormously since then. Speeches in commendation of Mr. George wers also delivered by Messrs. W. Summertield, J. Corradine, C. Pritchard, R. Entwistle, J. Gilman, R.Schofield, Cottrell, C. Bromley, and R. Lawton, and Mrs. Baxter. The programme was varied during the evening by recitations and songs as follows: Glee, Mrs. Brough, and Messrs. Bailey and Entwistle ; recitation, " Punch in the pre- sence of the passager," Mr. C. George ; song, " The Zuyder Zee," Miss Alice Morrell ;" recitation, "1101) and his wife Sally," Mr. F. Hardy ; comic song,Mr. T. Lewis George. Mr. T. Brough accompanied during the musi- cal part ef the programme. A vote of thanks to the chairman and all who took part in the meeting brought a very enjoyable meeting to a close.

The actual work of constructing the 'Zomba- Salisbury section of Mr. Rhodes's great transcon- tinental line of telegraph, which the Cape Premier hopes may some day connect Cairo and Cape Town, has been commenced, and at the end of last, year some thirteen or fourteen miles had been put up at the Nyassaland end of the section. An appeal for help made to the Makololo chiefs by the engineers in charge resulted in some two hundred men being , set to work to make the " telegraph road," and very , shortly as many as six hundred men were employed in erecting the posts and carrying the material. Naturally, the chiefs expect some acknowledgment, of their goodwill in this matter, and the agents of the Company begged them to say what form they would prefer that their presents should take. It, was a miscellaneous list which the chiefs forwarded, including as it did tea, sugar, jam, butter, and bis- cuits. But the demands were by no means c?,nfined to edibles. One request was for " clothes for women," another for a rifle for shooting hippopo- tamuses and one request—probably from an ex- student of the Blantyre Mission schools—was for " writing materials." It is stated that of the youn- ger men among the Makololo chiefs three are able to road and write, having acquired their ,education : at the Mission schools.

The late Earl of Bective was richly endowed with this world's treasures. His personal estate was proved last week at seven hundred and seventy-five thousand six hundred and 'fifty-se-veil pounds, nine shillings and eleven pence. A few trivial legacies will be deducted from this immense sum, and the remainder, as well as valuable estates in Westinere- land,go to the earl's only child,Lady Henry Bentinck. Few families have been so fortunate in their marri- ages as the Bentincks. The original Portland es- tates were very extensive ; but the greater portion of the property nevi in their possession was acquir- ed through -matrimonial alliance i with heiresses. Lord Henry Bentinck,who is the Duke of Portland' brother, has ilad his share of the family luck. He was married only a few years ago.

SOME NOTES FROM MY

READING.

BY MR. JOHN PIERS. "That," said my father, "depends very much on the mental constitution and requirements of the thinkers, as to what ansWer shall be given to this question. The conservative mind—the stickler for old forms, ceremonies, and iitual—will deplore the fact that the old order has to give place to the new; while the more advanced thinker will welcome such changes as are inevitable and, in his estima- tion, beneficial. And he will welcome this,however much it may interfere with vested inters:4a of a profession or whatever mischief it may work with its dogmas. Hear again what, our American says: For it is not to be concealed that the Euglibli Church is a gentlemanly institution. It not only teaches the lower orders deference to superiors, but its influence does much to breed that very admir- able character, the English gentleman. Its teach- ings are wholly at variance with the spirit of social democracy. Its very catechism inculcates a content which is opposed to the restless and pushing ten- dencies of modern times. The calechumen is made to say, among other things, when asked what is his to his neighbour, My duty to my neighbour is . . to submit myself to all my governors, teachers, spiritual pastors and masters.; to order myself lowly and reverently to a:1 my betters . . . and to learn and labor truly to get mine own living, and to do My duty in that state of life to which it shall please God to call me.' But now it seems to be the accepted duty of every man of English blood, no matter on which side of the great ocean he may be, to get himself with what speed he may, out of the sphere of life in which he is, unto a better. The virtue of content is gone,amtwith it the grace of submission. I remember intuitions of this even in my boyhood as I repeated those words, and vainly strove to reconcile them with the struggle for ad- vancement which I saw going on around ine,among even the most religious people. And there was the old story in verse which began :

• Hontst John Tomkias the hedger and ditcher. Although he was poor did not want to be richer.'

Honest John Tomkins was held up to me as the model of all the Christian virtues ; and yet I saw everybody around me, including my teachers and spiritual pastors and masters, striving by day and by night to be richer. And when we consider that discontent is the mother of improvement, whether for the individual or the community, and that the betters of the man who is taught to order himself lowly and reverently to thew, becomes so, because they or their ancestors were not satisfied with that state of life to which it had pleasied God to call them, is it not plain that the religion which teaches content is doomed, and with it the whole system Of governors and masters spiritual and temporal ? But it will be a long while before this warfare is accomplished. Not easily nor quickly can a form of society, which is of such slow and sturdy growth as that ot Ragland, and whose roots, like those of some vast British oak, decayed and hollow at heart It may be,yet pierce the mould of centuries. There is much in England that is mere shell and seems mere sham; but then that shell was shaped from within by living substance, and it has hardened into form through the sunshine of hundreds of years; and so it stands, and will yet stand long—although not for ever. The very shams and surface shovss of ',kings in England are strong and durable.'"

"Very candid of him," said Eusebins, " to tell us of the sturdy persistence of our shams. Of course, were they not persistent, it would not give the young shams time to grow to perfection and the breed would die ou.t,leaving the Yankees that much less to jeer at us about." "Don't think we need tear a failure in that crop"! quietly put in lir. Shandy. "But I ani of opinion that were there more out- spokenness,these shams and makebelieves would be !ewer and shorterlived. nod al/ the people who leal with shams the candour of Dr. Monierie that' reign would be a much shorter one."

UNIVERSITY EXTENSION LECTURES.

LECTURE AT RUNCORN.

On Monday evening, Mr. M. R. Sadler, M.A., deli- vered his popular lecture en " Oxford: Its history and great men," in the Public Hall, Ituncorn,before a large audience. The proceeds are to be given to the Extension Lectures Comtaittee, for the puepose of extinguishing the debt incurred during the last two courses of the Oxford University extension lec- tures. The chair was occupied by Canon Maitland Wood. The Chairman in opening the preceedings,thanked the audience for their presence and the interest they had showed in the credit and honour of the town of Run- corn. They would probably be all aware that the lec- ture was specially designed to clear off the responsibi- lity to Oxford for the University extension lectures, and thanks were due to every body who was helping them in their endeavours to be honest. In Runcorn they had a local interest in Oxford. Probably everyone was aware that since the Reformation the three great parishes of laudwortia, Frodsham, and Runcorn had really paid their greaa tithe to Christ Church, and in that way had been the means of promoting good, sound, religious, and scholarly learein,g at that great place of instruction. Not only had they that connection with the University of Oxford, but besides that, Christ Church had sent a vicar to those parishes for at least 200 years. The col- lege had played so great a part in the history of England that it was difficult .for any thoughtful Englishman not to be interested in the place. '1 he outside view of Ox- ford was very different when he was a young man,trom what it was now. Public opinion was very much in- fluenced by a book ." Mr. Verdant Green," the remarks wherein were considered to be something right. Oxford was looked upon as being a very exclusive place, and famous for its very dry orthodoxy, one that was politic- ally of the fos:il Tory order,and also famous for its good port wine. (Laughter.) Tney had changed very much. Oxford was now very different. Instead of being ex- clusive,the university was opening its arms to the whole of the country and doing all it possibly could do to inte- rest the people in other towns. Instead of a very dry orthodoxy, he ventured to say that there were scarcely any religious views which did not find an opportunity of flourishing, and instead of being Tory there was scarcely a movement in the world but a very old Oxford Radical was at the head.and he did riot think they even saw port wine. The interest in Oxford must be wider than in the past, and Mr. Sadler was one who was con- nected with the university,with Christ Church,and with the extension mqvement. (Applause.) . The Lecturer said that the University was founded some 1,100 years ago by the daughter of St. Frideswyde, who refused to enter into the high marriage intended for her, but resolved to devote her life to the nursing of the sick. Ile went on to review the various schools and principal leaders in connection with the University,and referred to Oxford as being a place where the Jews were considered safe, and this made it a place of wealth. The trades and industries were organised, and the scholars . desiring to attain for their society, local recognition, or- ganised themselves on similar lines, and thus it was that they, like craftsmen entered into a sort of apprentice- ship for seven years, and on matriculation, they became Masters of Arts. He alluded to the riotous conduct of the students during the thirteenth century, and said - that through them, considerable municipal powers were lost. He spoke of the Franciscans, Friar Bacon, John Wycliffe, and the Lollards, Cardinal Wolsey and Arch- bishop Loud, and the condition of Oxford during the civil war. He also described the state of Oxford in the eighteenth century. and also alluded to the teaching of John Wesley, remarking that to whatever denomination they might belong to. they could not help but, admire the self sacrifice and courage of that man and his brother, who had done so much to found Methodism. He next alluded to the immense influence John New- man and the Tractarian movement had upon Oxford. In 1850 Parliament took the first steps towards remov- ing the religious tests, so that members of any religious denomination could enter into studies of the University, and the result was, that the institution was brought in- to touch with the whole people. He concluded his lec- ture with the account of the formation of local examin- ations, and the accommodation for poor scholars, and also for women students, also the sending out of lec- turers. These things had recovered f)r Oxford that high position which it had previously occupied in the nation, and had endeared it more closely to the hearts of the people. These changes had been to a very great extent brought about throughillie instrumentality of the Hon. Mr. Toynbee and John Ruskin. A vote of thanks was accorded to the lecturer on the proposition of Air. Holland, seconded by 'Mr. William - Handley, both of whom spoke in high terms of the inter- esting lecture, which they said was an intellectual treat which had been given to them. In responding the lec- turer called for a vote of thanks to the Cbairman,which was heartily gi,ven.

THE SILT UNION.

AN UNSATISFACTORY YEAR.

At the fifth annual meeting of the Salt Union, held in London on Tuesday-. the chairman kMr. Lionel Ashley) said the result of the year's working had been disap- pointing, but he thought they might reasonably look forward to better times. It might be necessary for the Union, in conjunction with other salt makers, to limit the production, and the directors were quite prepared to consider any schema which would ensure fair prices. A dividend at the rate of 3 per cent on the ordinary shares was deeared.

WARRINGTON BUILDING SOCIETIES.

The second annual meeting of the Warrington and District Perfect Thrift Building Society was held in the Academy-street Mission Room on Mon- day evening. Mr. J. J. Nield was voted to the chair. After the minutes of the last annual meeting had been confirmed, the Chairman, in the course of his remarks, congratulated the members on the result of the year's work. Considering the disturbance , in trade, and the unprecedented distress resulting there- from, the progress made, he thought, was very satisfac- tory, and proved that the society was in a very flourish- ing condition. The report and balancc sheet of the direc- tors and auditors were submitted and passed. Messrs. Dell, Dunbobbin, Clayton, Crompton, and Dalton were elected directors for the ensuing year. Mr. Edmund Osborne was elected auditor. At the close of the meeting a ballot for £lOO took place. Messrs. Cook and Mason were elected scrutineers, and Master Charles Woods was selected to draw the ballot balls, the result being that the share No. 420, held by Mr. John Danoghue, was drawn and entitled him to the use of £lOO free of in- terest for 16i years. After a hearty vote of thanks had been passed to the Chairman and scrutineers, a very successful meeting was brought to a close. The fourth annual meeting of the Third War- rington . Starr Bowkett Building Society was held in the Legh-street schoolroom. on Tuesday evening. The chair was taken by Mr. J. S. Garland, one of the directors of the society, who was pleased to be able to present such a good balance sheet. He thought this was especially satisfactory when they remembered what a commercially disastrous year the past twelve months had been throughout the country. He hoped that in his time there would be no repitition of anything like the great coal strike of 1893. (Applause.) Turning to the details of the annual report, he announced that the total receipts were £1,287 14s. 91,0 f which sum -£933 Es. were members' subscriptions. This., he remarked, was an improvement upon last year's receipts. During the year seven appropriations had been made. There were now 331 members. The minutes of the third annual meeting, held in February 1893, were then read and passed ; after which the present balance sheet was passed as read. The four directors Messrs. J. S. Gar- land, El. Ferguson, D. Fanshaw, and Wm. Parkinson, who were retiring according to rule, were unanimously re-elected, as were the retiring auditors, M essrs. Walsh and Timperley. It was proposed and carried that the auditors fees be the same as before, viz. ,L 3 3s. between the two. The general business having been concluded, the 22nd appropriation by ballot took place. The num- bers 264 and 243 were drawn, .but as the members hold- ing those numbers had not fully paid up their sub- scriptions they were not butit 1 ed to the advance. Finally number 144 was drawn, and Mr. John Harrison, of Stockton Heath, the holder of that number, is entitled to an advance of £4OO free of interest for 12i years. Messrs. Oddie and Horne acted as scrutineers, and the balls were drawn by Gilbert Simcock. The proceedings terminated with a vote of thanks ta the chairman and scrutineers.

INDUSTRIAL SCHOOLS AND THE

ENLISTMENT OF BOYS.

MR. BRUNNER, M.P., AND THE HOME SECRETARY.

At a meeting of the School Attendance Committee of the Northwich Union on Friday, last week, the clerk (Mr. A. Fletcher) reported that he had communicated with Mr. Brunner, M.P., the member of the division, relative to the action of the managers of the Standon Bridge Industrial School in enlisting a boy without the consent of his parents or the authority at whose instance he was sent to the school. Mr. Brunner replied as follows :

9, Ennismore-gar&n,s, S.W., 12th February, 1894. Dear Mr. Fletcher,—l had an interview with Mr. Asquith this evening on the subject of your letter of the 10th. He received me very kindly, told me that the case of the boy Alfred Palin had come before him personally, that he had considered it very carefully, and that he sincerely regrets his entire want of legal power to inter- fere. He reminded me that he had expressed himself strongly in the House of Commons some months ago upon a similar case, in which the master of a reforma- tory ship had received a fee for the enlistment of a boy in a regimental band. Mr. Asquith said that, in his opinion, the managers of the Standon Bridge Industrial School have full legal right to act as they did in this case (under the Act 1891), and he regrets that such a power should have been conferred upon them. lam glad to hear from you that my colleagues on the School Attendance Committee agree with Mr. Asquith that the action of the managers of the Standon Bridge School is greatly to be regretted, and I cordially recommend them, as well as my brethren on the bench, never again to send a boy to an industrial school without first obtaining from the managers an assurance that he will not be enlisted without the consent of the local authority and of tho parents being first obtained.—Believe me, yours faithfully, JNO. T. BRUNNER.

The committee instructed the clerk to thank Mr. Brunner for his letter, and also decided that they could take no further action in the matter.

NORTIIWICH DIVISION LIBERAL ASSOCIATION. ANNUAL MEETING OF COUNCIL.

The annual meeting of the council of the above association was held in the reading room of the Gladstone Club, Northwich, on Saturday afternoon. Mr. H. Brett, C.C., presided, and there was a large attendance. Delegates were present from North- wich, Leftwich, Castle-Northwich, Winnington, Runcern, Barnton, Winsford, Wharton, Middlewich, Da venham, Moulton, Halton, Weaverham, &c. Mr. W. Handley (secretary) presented his annual re- port as follows :—A comparatively short period having elapsed since the date of our last annual meeting, it necessarily follows that a view of the subsequent work of our association is of a nacre limited character than usual. Our immediate work has been mainly confined to registration, increased electoral facilities, and an im- proved financial basis. Consistent with our usual prac- tice our registration was largely attended to, previous to the issue of the new voters' lists; but it is only fair to state, and indeed satisfactory to acknowledge, that year by year the assistant overseers of the townships coin- i prised in the division prepare and present their lists in Icomoleter form, thus evidencing their genuine desire to place on the lists all who are qualified by occupation to be possessed of the franchise. It is, therefore, almost impassible to correctly tabulate the amount of work done, as from the system adopted our work in the Re- vision Court is comparatively light, and the result, as party registration agents, is rather to be looked for in the regular outcome of the ballot box. During the year our polling districts have been increased by four, or from -12 to 16, the additions being at Leftwich, Winnington, Wharton and Weston, thus bringing the convenience of voting much nearer the homes of the electors in those districts. These alterations and additions were cordially, acquiesced in by the Conservative party as represented by their able agent, Mr. C. J. , Hughes. who conjointly assisted to the uttermost in shewing the necessity for the wisdom of these additional facilities being granted. In accordance with the state- ment of our treasurer at the last annual meeting, a meeting of representatives of each polling district was convened shortly afteewares to consider the placing of the association finances on a simpler and more automa- tic basis, at which meeting a scheme of polling district a.seessinent, to be under the superintendence of each dis- trict council, was drawn up and remitted to the various districts for consideration. At a subsequent executive meeting the scheme was unanimously adopted. and so far as the returns have to the present come in from the polling districts, the scheme is not only feasible and easy of accomplishment, bet will, if faithfully carried out do all that is expected of it. It will naturally take a little time for the change and its advantages to be thoroughly understood and appreciated, but we may reasonably hope that our next annual meeting will wit- ness its being loyally in full operation. The inordinately long parliamentary session of 1893, which is not yet ended, prevented the holding of those public meetings usually taking place each autumn, at which our mem- ber was always welcomed on presenting himself to give an account of his stewardship, but as one index, if one at all is needed, of the faithfulness of Mr. Brunner's ser- vices, the Government division list issued last month places Mr. Brunner's name as fourth of the twelve mem- bers representing Cheshire, which, taking into account that he has been laid aside for a time by sickness, may be considered as eminently satisfactory, more especially .as his vote as always been cast in agreement and sym- pathy with the wishes and opinions of the majority of his constituents. Turning to the wider political horizon, we hear of" wars and rumours of wars." and as an as- sociation we can be no indifferent spectators of the Pending conlict. We see, and not for the first time,the wishes of the people thwarted by the peers, the work of the representative flouted by the unrepresentative, liberty and freedom confronted and checked by privi- lege, the realization of those reforms in anticipation of which the struggle was so keen at the last election hin- dered and delayed ; and unless we as a party are content "to lick the dust," it is our bounden duty, as it will be our privilege, to be prepared and ever on the alert to take a foremost part in that nearing conflict which must for once and all decide that a free people must rule in al free country." (Applause.) Mr. J. T. Years:eye-tweed the adoption of the report, observing that he did not think it was possible to re- ceive a more satisfactory one. (Applause.) Mr. Peter Foy (Northwich), seconded, and the motion , was unanimously carried. Mr. E. Milner, C.C. (hon. treasurer), said they would remember that at the last meeting he had to report that they were £135 in debt, or rather that they had £135 of liability. It was then resolved that a meeting should take steps to place the finances on a satisfactory basis. This had been done, and a scheme was prepared which had been confirmed by the Executive Committee. It was that each district should contribute in proportion to the number of voters. He (the speaker) was pleased to have been enabled to put such a satisfactory statement before them. (Applause.) On the proposition of Mr. J. Dodd (Winsford), the accounts were adopted. Mr. J. Mclntyre, of Belmont, moved the following re- appointment; or appointments : Mr. E. H. Moss, C.C., Middlewich, president; Mr. Philip Speakman, C.C., Runcorn, chairman of council ; Mr. H. Brett, C.C., Northwich, chairman of executive ; Mr. E. Milner, C. 0., Winningten, bon. treasurer ; and Mr. A. Fletcher,North- wich, hon. secretary. Hulse (Wins- ford), T. Hulrne (Middlewich), Joseph Millington (Middlewich), H. Bratt (Northwich), H. Capes (Winning- ton). T. Rayner (Winchans), J. M. Fells (Winsford), A. R. Norman (Runcorn), T. INDEPENDENT LABOUR PARTY.

AN ELECTORAL CAMPAIGN.

A meeting of the Administrative Council of the Inde- pendent Labour Party was held at Manchester on Mon- day, Mr. J. Keir Hardie, M.P., presiding. The salary of Mr. T. Mann as secretary was fixed at £l5O a year. In view of the next general election it was decided to raise a bind of £20,000 for the expenses of Labour can- didates,and a committee was formed to give efrfrct to the resolution. It was announced that there were 17 Labour candidates already in the field, and ten or twelve others would be adopted at an early date. A resolution was passed disclaiming all connection with the principles and motives of the klarchists in Constitutional coun- tries. The proceedings were marked by much en- thusiasm.

THE SATURDAY EVENING CONCERTS.

The usual Saturday evening concert was held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, on Saturday evening. There was a very large attendance. Tile Rev. J. Yonge occupied the chair and delivered a short address. He said he was very much afraid that he would not be able to satisfactorily fill up the item on the programme of chairman's address unless, indeci, the exceeding brevity should be a very satisfactory item to the audience. He was of opinion that at a meeting of that kind, the chairman's address was the one thing that was not wanted. He, however, gave his cordial approval to that movement. Through the winter season which was now far advanced, that room had been filled every Saturday evening with a large audience, who had enjoyed very muck the programmes which had been provided for them. He hoped and trusted that those entertainments might be but the beginning of a series of entertainments in the winters to come. (Applause.) It was a very desirable thing to introduce and develop their own local talent, and it was a very great source of gratification that they were pos- sessed of a very considerable amount of local talent I which could be used at those concerts. He was glad to know that those who had been catering for them were , willing to cater for them in the future. These enter- tainments were held under the auspices of a new institu- tion under the name of the Social Questions Union. He would like all present to understand that the Social Questions Unions was not quite the thing which some people seemed to think it was. It was thought by someto be an instrumentality for making people uncomfortable, for stirring up unpleasant questions, but the general object was the social improvement of the people in that town. (Applause.) The Union was working very quietly at present but h€ believed in the time to come it would make a desirable improvement in the town. (Applause.) The programme was then proceeded with, and proved to be of an enjoyable character. Mr. Mills provided the programme and it was as follows :—Glee, "The Village Blacksmith," Mrs. Monks, Miss Ashley, Messrs. Hassall and Pownall ; duet, " Tired," Mrs. Monks and Miss Ashley; song, "The dumb wife," Mr. Mills; song, Mr. Heald; recitation, "The rolling of the drum,' Miss Harrison; duet, "Excelsior," Messrs. Hassall and I Pownall ; recitation, " God bless you," Mr. Whitfield ; song, " Killarney," Mrs. Monks; song," Another Day," Miss Ashley; recitation, "The Fireman's wedding," Miss Harrison ; song, "London Tower," Mr. Pownall ; song, " Be true to me," Mr. Mills; duet, Miss Ashley and Mr. Hassall; and song, "Love's Request," Mr. Hassall. The whole of the items were well rendered and encores were frequent during the evening. The Rev. J. Watkins, Primitive Methodist minister, Liverpool, was to have addressed the meeting hut he did not put in an appear- ance. A vote of thanks was passed to the Chairman on the proposition of Mr. S. B. Stringer.

. A BURN LEY MIRACLE.

INSTANT RELIEF AFTER SEVEN YEARS' SUFFERING FROM INDIGESTION.

(From the " Burnley Gazette.")

In the district of Hollingreave, Burnley, gossip has b6en very busy for a week or two concerning the re- markable expetience of a young lady in that neighbour- hood. The reports were so presistent and astonishing that a representative of the" Burnley Gazette " deter- mined to make inquiries on the spot and see for himself whether there was anything in it. Proc,seding at once to Glebe Terrace, in Dall-streot, he inquired for the resi- dence of Miss Fanny Parry and was smilingly invited in by the young lady who answered his knc,ck, and who proved to be the identical person he was in search of. Her beaming face, pleasant manner, and robust appear- ance seemed anything but consistent with a long spell of suffering, and our interviewer ventured to drop a hint I in that direction.

"Yes," she replie3, " everybody is saying my word, how well you look; what have you been taking?' I couldn't tell you how different I feel. It is certainly no exaggeration to call it a miracle." In answer to the reporter's inquiry, Miss Parry told the whole story of her suffering and relief therefrom. " It is now seven years," she said, " since I was first attacked by indigestion. I was then nineteen years old; the disease gradually increased from that time, and for the last two years I can assure you I have suffered cov, stantly, and never expected to be well again. Often for days together I 'could eat nothing, and then, when actually starved into taking a little, it caused such pain that I didn't know what to do with mycelf. I was some- times a week and scarcely had a wink of sleep. I was weary when' went bed and weary wlan. I got up." "Did you never see what a doctor could do for

you ?" "Oh, yes, I had a doctor attending me„ but he didn't make any difference. I was off my work for three months at one time, and again for three weeks just before last Christmas. I had a doctor to me then, but didn't get any relief. Then I've tried almost everything I heard about." " And did nothing give you relief ? "

"No ; I have had herbs and all sorts of things which different people recommended, or which I had read about, but all to no purpose, until I happened to see in one of the papers an account f a case which had been cured by Dr. Williams' Pink Pllls for Pale People. Some- one working beside my sister at the mill also recom.. mended roe to try them, and just before Christmas, after I had been off my work for three weeks, I started taking them. This was en the Sunday, and the effect was so immediate that I actually went to my work on the Mon- day and have been working ever since." "Do you mean that they cured you straight away ?" "Yes, I seemed to find relief first thing, and I have never had the least trouble since. I went on taking them for a few weeks, one after each meal. I can eat any- thing now, and enjoy my meals, which I was unable to do before for years. It's a blessing to work now instead of being a burden."

"And do you ascribe all this to Dr. Williams' Pink Pills?"

" Yes ; and I have recommended them to a lot of people since. They have made a wonderful difference to me. I was just like a skeleton before, and now all the folks I mix with are telling me how well I look and how plump Fm getting." The above story was corroborated by members of the family, and by Mr. James Pate, of 55, Briercliffe-road, who stated that only a few weeks ago the girl was quite thin and had a very sallow complexiou—in striking contrast to the sound, healthy finks, plump fi2;ure, and generally vigorous and robust appearance she has to-day. The girl's family were quite ela,ted with the remarkable change in her condition. It is not alone in cases of indigestion that Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People produce these remarkable effects. They are recognised as one of the greatest modern medicines, a perfect blood builder and nerve restorer, curing such diseases as rheumatism, neuralaa, partial paralysis, locomotor ataxy, St. Vitus' dance, ner- vous headache, nervous prostration, and the tired feel- ing resulting therefrom ; diseases depending upon humours in the bond, such as scrofula, chronic erysipe- las, &c. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills restore pale and sallow complexions to the glow of health, and are a specific for all the troubles peculiar to the female sex, while in the case of men they effect a radical cure in all cases arising from mental worry, over-work, or excesses of whatever nature.

These Pills are manufactured by tilt,. BRITISH REVERSE IN AFIRICA.

A telegram was received on Monday in London from Rear Admiral Bedford, Commander-in-Chiet or the Afri- can West Coast statisn, giving details of the British re- verse. He states that Captain Gamble, with two hun- dred officers and men from the Raleigh, Widgeon, and Magpie, advanced on Birlthatna, destroyed two stock- aded villages, and then returned to the coast. The tide was too low to enable the boats to approach the shore, s,nd the enemy attacked our men, tiring at them from cover, and inflicting heavy loss. Fourteen men wen', killed, and several dangerously wounded. The despatch says that all showed the greatest gallantry and coolness, I a splendid example being set by the officers. The Queen telegraphed on Monday to the First Lord of the Admir- alty expressing her grief at th, reverse and hersytnpathy with the relatives of the killed nd also with the wounded.

MONDAY.

In the House of Commons on Monday evening Mr.Mhx- DELLA. informed Mr. Charming that he proposed to intro- duce next session a bill based on the report of the Select Committee on railway rates. Mr. ACLAND, answering questions about charges for school-books in which a reference was made to the Man- chester School Board, said a parent could not be com- pelled to pay for the use of school-books. -Sir H. ROSCOE asked the Vice President of the Council if he had seen a letter written, by direction of a teacher, by a child attending St. Stephen's School, Chorlton-on- Medlock, asking her parents to pay a subscription to school funds in lieu of the remitted school fees. Mr. Aenalin gave the text of the letter. He said it was evident that the letter was not very spontaneous; To make children copy out what was really a circular soliei- ting subscriptions for the school seemed to him a very undesirable proceeding, and he would make further in- quiry into the case. Mr. GnahwroNransweringansweri a question, said he could not hold out any hope of the introciuction of a Scotch Home Rule Bill.

Sir U. KAY Sacti-msworrra, in reply to Lord G. Hamil- ton, said that so far as the Admiralty were aware there was no truth in the rumour of a second repulse of British forces on the West Coast of Africa. The Local Government (Parish Councils) Bill as re- turaed from the House of _Lords was considered. The Lord's amendment making a parish Council optional where the population was between 200 and 500 was re- jected by 212 to 161. The House agreed with the amend- ment providing that the hiring of laud should take place through county councils instead of through the Local Government Board. The Lord's amendment providing that in the case of parochial charities not ecclesiastical, the parish council or parish meeting should appoint rot a majority of the governing body but only a third was thrown out by 196 to 139; but Sir W. HARCOURT assented to the substitution of " may " for " shall," and an amendment to this effect was agreed to without a divi- sion. Reasons for disagreeing with amendments,madat by the Lords baying been agreed to, the House adjourned until Thursday. The House of Lords had a short sitting, at which the Earl of KI3IDERLEY suggested au adjournment " during pleasure—in order that the House might receive the Commons' amendments to the Local Government Bilk— land a meeting on Tuesday to consider such amendments. The Marquis of SALISBURY moved that the House at its rising should adjourn until Wednesday, and this 'lntim was carried by 58 to 16.

WEDNESDAY.

The House of Lords considered en Wednesday evening the Commons latest amendments to the Local Govern- ment (Parish Councils) Bill. The Marquis of SALISBURY moved an amendment that 300 (instead of 200) should be the limit of population for the compulsory establish- ment of a parish councii,and the amendment was carried by 60 to 55. The noble marquis then moved that the House should insist on its amendment to the clause dealing with the administration of parochial charities, with an alteration, however, which would leave it to the Charity Commiseioners to decide what number of trus- tees the parish should appoint. The Earl of Kimberley said such a provision would be distasteful to the agri- cultural community, but on a division Lord Salisbury's amendment was carried by 83 to 51. After some fur- ther discussion the bill as amended was ordered to be returned to the House of Commons. In reply to the Earl of CAMPERDOWN, the Earl of KIAIRERLEY said an answer given by the Premier in the otner House made it clear that the GovernMent did not intend to concur in the suggestion for the appointment of a Joint Committee of the two Houses on the subject " betterment." Lord Salisbury thought a reply to the proposal of the House should have been given directly,in accordance with cus- tom,and said the Premier had apparently sought to show as much discourtesy to the Lords as he could—an impu- tation which Lord Kimberley resented.

THURSDAY.-MR. GLADSTONE AND THE HOUSE OF LORDS.

In the House of Commons, on Thursday evening, Sir Wu. HencoußT informed Mr. Whiteley that the Government had no intention of making a proposal for the re-assembling of the International itcnetary Confer- ence.

On the motion for the consideration of the Lords' amendments to the Local Government (Parish Councils Bill, Mr. GLADSTONB made a statement of the highest importaoce. He said that two amendments to the Local Government Bill were made in the House of Lords on Wedeesday, and in regard to both of them the Government strongly dissented from the House of Lords. The operation of sending the bill back- wards and forwards between the two Houses bad continued toe long, and the Covernment had coucluded that this operation Nhould cease, and that they should -adopt a decisive course. The question 'whether the majority of the representatives of the parish were to have commanding control over the charities was to the Government a question of principle. They acquiesced in the loss of an important bib the other day on account of an objectionable amendment, but the reason why they took that course was because they' thought the oh- objectionable clause vitiated all the work of that bill. In the present case the mischief done by the amendment was limited to a certain portion of the purposes of the bill, and under the circumstances the Government had arrived at the conclusion that it would not be well to wreck the whole work of the scssimeand had determiaed to advise the House to accept these amendments. They looked, however, to an early re versa of the unfortunate decision arrived at by the House of Lords. These amendments and the treatment of other hills of great importance which the House of Commons had Feat to the House of Lords raised a ques- tion of the gravest character. Tne question was whether the judgment of the House of Lords was not merely te mouify but to annihilate the work of the House of Com- mons, and the Goveroment acze mpanied the acceptance of these a mendmente with the sorrowful declaration that the difference between the Rouse of Lords and the House of Commons appeared to have reached a development such as to create a state of things of which they were compelled to say that in their judgment it could not continue. It was for the House of Commons to pro- nounce a judgment on this subject. But the Houae could not act as fluid judge in a case in which it was a party. There was a higher authority than the House of Com- mons, and that was the authority of the nation, which must in the last resort decide. The time when that judgment was to be invited was one which the Executive' Government of the day could alone consider and decide. In cnisidering these ameridreents they were considering an essential and inseparable part of a ques- tion which had become profoundly acute—a question which would demand a settlement, ani must receive at an early date that settlement from the highest authority Mr. BALFOUR accused the Preinier of making war against the Conetit ution, and said he wished for nothing better than to see the supporters ef the Government attempting to stir up the country on the issue which would be put before it—whether the House of Lords was right og was not right in vetsieg the Lleme Rule Bill. After a few remarks from Lord R. Churchill tito House proceeded te consider the Lord's amendments. Mr. Storey called for a division on the amendment as to parish charities, when there vote 1---tor agreeing with the Lords' amendment, 273 ; against, 37. The amendments were thus aceeptai, and the House aijourned till Monday, when the prorogation will take plsce: The House of Lords held a brief sitting to receive the result of the proccediege in the lieuse or C.immons err the Local Government Bill.
