THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY, MARCH 3, 1894.

LIB shareholders of the ship The Ship Canal Company who held their Canal.

half-yearly meeting in Manches- ter, on Wadnesday, were by no means disposed to take a gloomy view of the future of the great pro- perty in which they are concerned. Therehave been many prophets of evil with regard to the Canal, but already two of the most disquieting predictions have been completely falsified. One of these predictions was that the Canal would never be completed—ix fact, could nevar be com- pleted; the other was that if the Canal were made no large ocean-going vessel would ever venture along it. However, since the shareholders pre- viously met together they have had unirepeachable evidence of the completion of the Canal,. and of the fact that ocean-going vessels of large dimensions can find their way to the port of Manchester. The candid friends of the shareholders, dislodged from their original position, now assure them that they need never expect any dividends upon the capital that has been expended in making the Canal. This is an assertion which, in the very nature of things, cannot as yet be refuted;, but the share- holders, or at all events the larger number who put in an appearance at the meeting on Wednes- day,appear to be cheerfully optimistic. The Cana has been open for traffic about eight weeks only, and,. according to the diroctor who preside d,Mr.J. K.BYTUBLL,II Much bettor comrpencement has been made than he over expected, Shipping traffic is not easily and all at once diverted from old routes, but a commencemer.t has been made in many directions. Largo consignments of cotton from America have already been unshipped at Munches, ter. Lines of ships are now running regularly between that city and the West Coast of Africa, the Mediterranean, the fruit ports of Spain, and many other European shipping centres, to say mythiug of the pretty er tensive coasting trwio that

is now in full swing. Other lines are about to bin- opened, and altogether Mr. BYTHELL, speaking from his own experiencoof the preliminarydifficul- %Rie that have to be encountered, declares that he is astonished that the Canal' Company should have got a footing in so many trades in so short a time. The export trade upon which a great deal depends, as shippers like to get "back-loads," is being energetically developed. Partington bids fair to become an important coal port before long. The large " tips " which have been erected there are busily occupied in loading vessels, and if the information of Mr.. MARSHALL ST/IVES be correct a contract has been made for sixty thousand tons ,of South Yorkshire coal to be shipped there during the next six months. Another coal port is being formed at Acton Grange near Warrington by Messrs. RICHARD EVANS and Co., and' it is expected that large quantities of coals from Haydock will be shipped at that point. Now that the Canal is being navi- gated manufacturers and traders are beginning to show a disposition to acquire land on the banks upon which to erect their works and warehouses, and all this ultimately means increased traffic. There are, on the other hand, some disap- pointments. The Canal Company have from the commencement found bitter enemies in some of the railway companies, and this hostility seemste linger in the slowness manifested by the London. and North-Western and the Lancashire and York- shire Companies to connect their system with the docks. But now that the Canal is in full opera- tion it is not likely that even the most powerful ,railway corporation can for long ignore so potent a factor in the commercial life of the district. Mr. BYTHELL reserved what was perhaps the most in- teresting of his announcements for the end of his, speech. It was that "the Queen intends to per- form the ceremony of opening the Canal if she is • able to do so." This information was conveyedin , a telegram from Sir HENRY PONSONRY,.. and it , may, therefore, be expected that the formal open- ing of the Canal will take place a few montbn s hence with all the distinction which Royalty can ' give it.

TUB manifesto which has bees Anmalmifpesotrotntissued by the Parliamentary Cons—

mittee of the Trade Unions Con- gress shows that the workmen of the country have no intention to stand psssively by while the House of Lords robs them of the Employers' Liability Bill. The appeal has been addressed to the whole of the industrial population of the United Kingdom, and,addressed as it is byworkmen to worktnen,there is little fear that it will not meet with the closestat- tention. It is a pithy document; there is no prolix- ity about it, but all the salient facts of the situa- tion created, by the Peers are brought out. The 'workers are reminded that for thirteen years past the Trade Unions Congress has annually by a, unanimous vote declared itself entirely opposed ,to the system of contracting out of an Act of Par- liament.. The Government have shown every' , desire to conform to these wishes, but their efforts, have been " absolutely frustrated by a body of irresponsible legislators acting on behalf of a few, industrial corporations" in which many of the mid " irresponsibles " are personally interested. The committee then very pertinently asks what use is the vote to workmen if, when by the use of that vote they have placed in power men who will give them what they want, a privileged few are permitted to make a mockery of the most ex- tended franchise and all representative authority "It is,"continues this spirited appeal to theworkmen of the country," now necessary for you to. decide, whether you will tamely submit to this contempt' tuous treatment, or that you will determine to teach the House of Lords that they cannot oppose your will with impunity. The present crisis affords you an excellent opportunity for driving home an effective lesson of resistance to insolent and, arbitrary power." A great national labour conference is then called to be held in London on Morel' 17, and every trade, labour and friendly society is urged to forthwith eject it& representa- tives to attend the conference. There can be Be doubt that this great gathering of the clans of labour, so to speak, will be a great and strising success. What little dissension there has been amongst workingmen in regard to contracting out is rapidly disappearing. The appeal to the selfish feelings of railway and other men who have ace't dent funds of their own has failed. These we° are declining to have any hand whatever in de- priving the great majority of their fellow work' men of the benefits of tha Liability Bill. At the. coming conference there will,. we are convinced, h. such a display of the solidarity of the labouring masses, upon this subject aat the Peers and theig eiders and abettors will be extremely glad to best a precipitate retreat from the position they hs`le taken up. A CLAIM FOR WAGES AT RUNCORN. At Runcorn County Court, on Tuesday, a claim was heard in which Walter Temperley, baker, sued Messrs. Lowe and Monk, provision dealers, Church-street, Run- corn, fol. £1 73.;wages due to him. It was shown that : claimant was engaged as a weekly servant, but after- wards was paid by piecework. Under nis condition he thought he was entitled to leave at any moment,but his masters retained the sum due t him because he left without notice, which he cl,d to start business for his brother-in-law.—El ward Monk, representing the defen- dants, said the custom of tint trade was that bakers should give a week's notice before leaving. In this the plaintiff failed.—The registrar, Mr. Jolliffe, who heard the c t se, gave judgment for the defead tuts. GOLLTER'S use over ten tons of Pion- every week.-41det. Ma. F. MACCABE AT RUNCORN, On Tuesday evening next Mr. Frederick Maceabe, the celebrated entertainer, will give one of his delightful performances in the Town Hall. " Begone dull care," is the title ef the entertainment, and there is no doubt that during the enterrainment the audience will experi- ence.the Words of the title, whilst enjoying Mr. Mac- Cabe's wonderful melange of songs, ventriloquism, and also the character delineations. The various items in the repertoire, are almost all the compositions of this remarkably original entertainer, and bear the impress of his widely recognized abilities. Some of the sketches such as " The wandering minstrel from the Kist End of London," as well as others which are specimens of fine ventriloquimi, have been pronounced at the most mas- terly delineations of pathos, humour, and vocal power, seen on the stage. Mr. MacCabe will be assisted by Mdlle. Minnie, who will personate the statue in the " Magic Statue," and in that capacity will pertorrn some of the most graceful dances in vogue in oriental countries. Mdlle. Mania is widely known in this character. .103EPH PRUMELIAD, Watchmaker and Jeweller, 51, High- et., Ituncorn.—Adtd. " SATURDAY NIGHT CONCERT AT WIDNES. _

The monthly meeting of ths Runcorn Commis- sioners was held at the Town Hall on Thursday morning when there were preeut hi issrs. S. Taylor (chairman) F. Wiswall,ll..l,Griee.W. Collier, Hand- ley, W. MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT.

The Medical Officer reported that the deaths registered during the past month were 19. Four of these were inquest cases. The average death rate equalled 111 for twelve months, and including the inquest cases, nine per thousand of the estimated population.

ADEMWA.TS AND BUILDINGS COMMITTEE. The Committee recommended that the plan showing deviation of drain in Windmill-street be approved. That application be made to the Cheshire County Coun- cil for an increased grant in aid of main roads. That an additional lamp be fixed in Heath-road near the foot- way alongside the railway leading to FrOdsham. That the plan of the trustees of the late John Janion for the erection of three cottages at Wickstone Hills be not ap- proved on the ground that they do not comply with the new Building Bye-laws. (The architect was not in pos- session of such Bye-laws.) That the plan of Messrs. P. Walker and Son for the erection of water closet and urinal at the Fleetwood Arms, Church-street, be not ap- proved because of their objectionable position. —The minutes were adopted on the motion of Mr. Wiswall.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT RUNCORN. '

BIC AN OCCASIONAL COILILBSPONDIINIL

The Liberals of Weston,Weston Point, and Clifton which townships have been formed into a polling district, have acted wisely in forming themselves into an association. They are to be congratulated upon their decision, and my hope is that success may attend the movement. This is scarcely to be doubted, considering that they have chosen as their President, Mr. James Handley, J.P., who is held in the highest respect and esteem in the district. The other officials are ardent workers in the cause of Liberalism.

An organisation of this description ought to exist in every village and hamlet, in order that the true principles promulgated by the party of pro- gress may be inculcated into the minds of the residents, so that they may be in a position to act wisely when called upon to exercise the franchise. I heartily commend them for their determination to commence a series of lectures, whereby they may be educated in the reforms as dear to the hearts of all true Liberals, and may be able to give a reason for their faith. I hear that already pro- mises have been received from several gentlemen to give addresses on up-to-date subjelts. If the meetings manifest the enthusiasm of the one last Friday evening progress is certain.

Whilst on the subject, attention may be drawn to the fact that the Lectures Committee of the Runcorn Liberal Club have arranged for Mr. Callia, of Liverpool, to deliver in that institution an ad- dress on " The French Eevolution and what it cost us," on Wednesday evening, in the club premises. The Speaker. should have made his appearance two or three weeks ago, hut, at the last moment found it impossible to fulfill his engagement. The subject is one deserving consideration, and I hope the meeting will be largely attended.

The meeting of the Northwich Division Liberal Association which took place on Saturday, was one of the most successful hitherto held in connection with the Council. The report submitted by Mr. W. Handley was of a highly encouraging descrip- tion, showing as it did that advancement was being made in every part of the division. The appoint- ment of officers demonstrates that Runcorn is very well represented, as it deserves to be seeing the stronghold it is to the Liberal cause. lar. P. Speakman, C.C., was appointed chairman of the Council, which is a compliment to him in con- sideration of his manifold services to the cause. There is every proof that the party are determined to continue supreme in the division.

I hear that the Wesleyans of Runcorn have given a chance to the School Board to take over their day schools. How the subject will be dealt with will probably be indicated at the next monthly meeting of that body. .The Board for home time have laboured under a dificulty through want of in- creased accommodation, hut whether this move- ment will remedy the want is difficult to say with accuracy. Many of .the departments have been full if not crowded at the Wesleyan schools.

The Tonic-sol-fa prize choir are to be congratu- lated upon the very successful production of " Jephtha," on Wednesday evening. The way in which the dloruses were interpreted is a striking proof of the ability of their conductor, Mr. John liolford, who has for many years bestowed much valuable time upon the training of those musically inclined. He has been the means of educating a very Milo number of students in the " art which strengthens the bonds of civilised society, humanizes and softens the feelings and dispositions of man, produces a refined pleasure in the mind, and tends to raise up in the soul emotions of an eaßed niture "

FORMATION OF A. LIBERAL ASSOCIATION AT WESTON POINT.

On Friday evening a meeting of the Liberals of Weston, Weston Point, and Cliton WaB held in the Catholic Schoolroom, Quarry Bank, Weston, for the purpose of forming a Liberal is.sociatiroi for the district. Over the f-int part •fi the proceedings Mr. Nesbitt, chairman of the council, presided, and among those preseat were Messrs. J. Handley, J P. (president), T. W. Pickering (secretary of the council), W. Epps (of the excutive council), T. Ravenscroft, J. Smith, S. Dodd, Wm. Tweddall, John Fraser, John Rowlands, P. Burrows, R. Collins, and J.Catterall (committee). There was a good attendqnce, especially considering the in- clemency of the weather.

The bt iairmatt explained that Weston had been formed into a polling district, comprising Weston, Wes- ton Point,and Clifton. This being so the advisability of the Liberals forming themselves into an association had been considered, and it had been decided to take that step. Accordingly a committee had been formed. In ' order to be associated with the Executive Council, they were called upon to give an annual subscription. After meeting this they required a fund to provide lec- tures each month during the winter, so that Liberals might be educated in their cause. Mr. Handley thanked the gathering for paving ap- pointed him as tte first president of their newly-forreed association, which promised to do so much for the ad- vancement of their noble cause. Liberalism meant good government for the people by the people themselves. Their associa•ion would be the means of giving valuable information on the principles they held dear. He, alluded to the effort to pass the Parish Councils Bill, whereby those residing in villages would have a larger measure of self-government. If the Bill became law they would. be better able to govern themselves,and thus they would take a deeper interest in local affairs. The vestry meet- ing hitherto had been a very tame .affair. The ratepayers had no voice in the expenditure of money. They were asked to nominate overseers, but the appointment was left in the hands of the magistrates. They made the poor rate, and it was the duty of the inhabitants to pay them. In the future,when the measure had been passed, their interest in the raiainrz and levying of -rates would be increased. The would also have in their hands the appointment of their own overseers. He urged as many as possible to join the association. A number of persons gave in their names as members, and afterwards Mr. John Smith was appointed on the Executive Council, to take the place of Mr. Epps, who was about to leave the neighbourhood. After the busi- ness had been tranca6ted a meeting was held under the presidency of Mr. James Handley, J.P., to heat an ad- dregs by Mr. B. Kniveton, of Runcorn, on" Social pro- blems, and their solutions."

IHALTH COMMITTSB.

This committee recommended that Mr.Handley be ap- pointed a meraber of the committee. That the applica- tion of Joseph Sauntlers for permission to carry on the bone boiling at the Old Smelting Works, Gas-street, be granted. That John Leach, Bold-st.. be registered as a purveyor ef milk in the place of Thomas Williams. That the house situate in Fryer-st., and oocupied by Daniel Reed be registered as a common lodging-house, number of lodgers 31. The Sanitary Inspector having reported that the premises situate Nos. 2 to 22 inclusive St. John- at., are without sufficient privy accommodation it is recommended that notice be served on the owner under section 38 of the Public Health bet, 1875, to abate the nuisance within 17 days of the service of the notice by pulling down the existing privies and ashpit and build- ing in place thereof four privies with ashpit furnished with proper doors and coverings, the ashpit to be venti- lated and made water-tight; and that in the event of non-compliance with such notice within the specified time the Board shall do the work thereby required to be done and recover in a summary manner from the owner , the expenses incurred by them in 60 doing. The Sani- tary Inspector having reported that a nuisance exists on premises situate Nos. 9, 10, and 11, Rock Mount, off Heath-road, arising from defective ashpit drains and pavement, it is recommend.ed that a notice be served on the owner to abate the nuisance within 14 days of the service of the notice and for that purpose to repair the privies and ashpit, repair the pavement and lay new earthenware socket pipe drain with stench trap,and that in the event of non-compliance with such notice legal proceedings be taken. The S unitary Inspector having reported that the owner of premises situate Nos. 61,62, and 64, Cooper-st., has converted the water closet into a peivy, it is recommended that notice be served on him, to refix the necessary water closet fittings, and put the sante in proper working order within fourteen days of the service of the notice, and in the event of non-compli- ance with such notice that legal proceedings be taken. The sanitary inspeetor having reported that a nuisance exists at premises situate Nos. 19 and 21 Fryer-fit., aris- ing from the proximity of privy and ashpit to the dwell- ing-house, it is recommended that notice be served on the owner to abate the nuisance within 14 days from the service of the notice, and for that purpose to substitute a water etc:met for the privy and two ash boxes for the ashpit, and that in the event of non-compliance with such notice legal proceedings be taken.— the minutes were passed on the motion of Mr. Liaison. MARKETS COMMITTEE. - PROPOSED INCRILASE OF lIALART.

This commttee recommended that the salary of the market superintendent be incrsased from £BO to £9O per annum, to take effect from the Ist of April next.—Mr. Mattison, in moving the adoption of the minutes, said that the Market Inspector had given every satisfaction, and the recommendation to increase his salary had been passed unanimously by the committee.—Mr. Grice se- conded and corroborated Mr. Hankinson's statement. —Mr.Bishrown moved as an amendment that the minute be referred back to the committee. He found nothing to say against Mr. Garrett personally, but he did not think that his salary she old be increased at present. THE RUNCORN TONIC SOL-FA. PRIZE CHOIR. GRAND CONCERT.

A grand concert was given in the Runcorn Public Tiell, on Wednesday evening by the Runcorn Tonic Snail Prize Choir, who went through the last ora- torio composed by Handel, viz., "Jephtha." There was a very large audience. The work is based upon the period of Jewish history when the jades ruled the country and the people had incurred the displeasure of God by their idolatrous practices. The soloists engaged for the concert on Wednesday were Mr. Thin Child, as Jephtha, captain and judge of the Israelites ; Miss Ravell, as Storge, his wife ; Miss Ada Lee, as Iphis, their d'eughter ; and Mr. A. S. Kinnell as Zebul, half-brother of Jephtha. Mr. Child took the place of Mr. Edwin Houghton, who, owing to a throat affection, was unable to fulfil his engagement. The principal item falling to Mr. Child's lot was, " Waft her, angels, through the skies," in which he appeared to ereat advantage inso- much that he received an encore. He possesses a very good tenor voice, and all his efforts were thoroughly appreciated. Miss Ravel made her first appearance before a Runcorn audience, and her rendering of the various recitatives and airs was extremely well interpreted. Her rendering of " Scenes of horror, scenes of woe," was loudly applauded. Miss Ada Lee fully sustained her previous high reputation, and she received an ovation for her rendition of "The smiling dawn of happy days," and "Tune the soft melodious lute." Mr. Kinnell, who is well known in this neigh- bourhood as an excellent artiste, had not very much to do, but what he did take part in, he showed his sterling merit. The choruses were given with remarkable pre- cision,ard the choir acquitted themselves to the eltire satisfaction of t'ee 13rge aticUince, They were enthu- siastically encored for the rendering of the chorus corn- meneirg "When is loud voice in thunder spoke." The choir have on the present occasion completely surpassed all their previous efforts, and they are to be congratu- lated upon the masterly execution of so dificult a work. In the band, there was a numher of members of Sir Charles Halle's orchestra, under the leadership of Mr. J. W. Collinson, and Mr. J. Holford wie:deel the hatou with efficiency.

RUNCORN AND THE DUES Q UESTION.

A DEPUTATION TO THE SHIP CANAL •DIRECTORS.

On Thursday a deputation from Runcorn waited upon' the Ship Canal Company at their offices,Spring Gardens. M anchester. The depthesti on consisted of Mr. Pritchard (e,bairman of the Parini mentary Com- mittee), Mr. P. Speakman, j.P., C.C., Mr. F. A. Lake (law clerk), Mr. F. A. Sznith, and Mr. Greenway :Millington (representing the shipping trade). Among those present were Mr. J. K. L's thell (chair man), Sir Joseph C. Lee, Mr. Galloway, Mr. Leader Williams, Mr. I.larsliall Stevens, Mr. .(Sr. ft. Collier, Mr. Riley (solicitor to the company), and Mr. Whit- worth (secretary). Mr. Pritchard introduced the deputation, after which Mr. Whitworth read the tollewing memorial of the sub- committee to the Runcorn Parliamentary and General Purposes Committee :—" Gentlemen,--The sob-commit tee appointed t draw up a statement of the grievances ued.r which the trade of Runcorn suffers, owing to the present charees of the Manchester Ship Canal Company beg to report as follows:- The dissatisfaction felt in Run- corn arises from the fact that the port does not appear to be fairly treated in the matter of dues and tolls (as they are published in the schedules of the Manchester Ship Canal Company), taking into consideration that the de- putation of the Commissioners that went to London in 1885 informed the traders that they had secured satis- factory terms, and further, that if any more favourable terms than they had obtained were granted to any other place, the traders in Runcorn would have granted to them equal terms; and this view of the matter we are of opinion is confirmed by the clause agreed upon between the Ship Canal Company and the deputation, and which clause is inserted in the Act of Parliament. Taking this view of the case, we wish to show to you that this is not being done, and that owing to dues and tolls being charged te trade of Runcorn is likely to suffer. Elles- mere Port has the right of free traffic on the canal in any kind of merchandise, providing that such merchandise is carried in vessels not exceeding 400 tons burden, and half dues and tolls if carried in vessels above 400 tons and under 800 tons. Runcorn, under present circum- stances, has but a very small share of such a right to the free use of the Canal, beyond what is granted to vessels coming light or in ballast, to load salt, or vessels that are loaded with salt ' • but in a circular issued from the Brideewater Offices, at Manchester, and dated January Ist. 1824, a concession is granted (until further notice) by which steamers not exceeding 400 tons burden, carrying exclusively materials to be used in the manufacture of pottery,which may come up to Runcorn by the canal, If they arrive in the Morsey upon tides which do not admit of their reaching Runcorn by the tideway, providing such materials are for convey- a,nce to the Potteries by the Company or their agents. But this concession does not apply to sailing vessels. If a sailing vessel of less than 400 tons carrying materials to be used in the manufacture of pottery, arrives in the Mersey on neap tides,the master or his agent must see the company's agent at Runcorn, and if he determines that the company are not ready to deal with the cargo, the vessel must stop at Liverpeol until such time as the company shall tow her to Runcorn by the tide- way. The Commissioners will see from this that Run- corn is placed at a disadvantage, and that the trade of the place is likely to suffer. Ellesmere Port has the tight of free use of the canal for all merchandise up to 400 tons. Runcorn has no such right, and Runcorn is now handicapped as a shipping port to the amount of such dues and tolls, whereas before the canal was made Runcorn was a better port than Ellesmere Port. In the schedule of tolls and wharfage rates it would appear from the reading of one of the clauses that ves- sels may take cargo destined for the Potteries district on to Manchester, and if the Company bring that cargo hack to any part of their premises, tolls are only charged as for the use of the last section used. Now, by taking some cargoes on te Manchester. say flints, which are not used as a rule for other than pottery purposes, a ship saves money and the trade of Runcorn suffers. We believe this is being done. Flints are being taken to Manchester, discharged into fie ts,and brought back from Manchester to Preston Brook, con- sequently our town loses that much trade, and the vessel saves paying dues which she would have to nay if she discharged at Runcorn. In the same schedule we find that if a vessel should load coals at Partington or Haydock, she has the advantage of having the use of the C nal for a toll of 6J. per ton and pays no dues, whereas if a veseel loads coal at Runcorn, which place is not half the distance up the Canal that Partington she has tie pay the same toll, 61. per ton, for using the Canal, and in addition, she must pay ship dues, according to schedule, the rate of which depends upon the vessel's destination. In the opinion of 'he Sub-Committee : (1). The Runcorn traders should have granted to them the same treatment in re- spect to ship dues and canal tolls $i are changeable to vessels trading to Ellesmere Port, under the con- di! ions stated in the Ship Canal Act. (2) That the exaction of full dues from vessels coming to or calling at Runcorn, while less dues are charged if a vessel comes to or ealls at WarrinetoN, and no dues whatever are levied i • respect to ve,sele going to or coming from Manchester, gives to these other places an undue prefer- ence, and if this was shown to the Ship Canal Company, seeing that they own the waterways and docks here, they would grant to Runcorn all the facilities necessary .to maintain, or posßihly to increase, the trade of the Janes Pritchard (chairman), William Hartle, G. Nfillingron, S. FT. Salkeld and F. A. Smith." —Mr. Pritchard then explained the claims put forward by Runcorn, namely, to be treated with the same equal- Ly as Port, au.l qaetel th.c azeaealent witle

Mr. Adamson and Mr. Rodlingtob,of 1885, for the pro- tection of the trade and port of Runcorn. A discussion followed from which it appeared that sundry charges were receiving earnest attention and that reductions were being made on vessels calling at Runcorn. A circular was being sent out to the effect that vessels coming to Runcorn in ballast to load low class goods would he only subj-cted to half dues. A further promise was m:tda that on the representation of any excessive charges being made they would be sub- mitted to the traffic committee, witb a view to their re- moval.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN

AT WARRINGTON.

Most of your readers, in addition to the Cairo- street congregation will, I believe, be pleased to read the following allusion to the Rev. F. K. Freeston, which I cull from the London letter of tie Liverpiol "Post" on Monday morning:—" Among the Unitarians of London no minister has come more decidedly to the front than the Rev. Frank Freeston of EMU Church, of Notting Hill Gate. Some Of the mem- bers of Mr. Freeston's congregation used LO worship at the old Unitarian chapel in Essex-street, Strand, and for several years the site on which the present handsome church with a tapering spire stands was occupied by a small building of corrugated iron. There was a large sprinkling of men in the congre- gation this morning. I was rather surprised to see flowers on the Communion table, a considerable suipliced choir, and the minister wearing a sur- plice with a stole. The service was liturgical. The lessons were read from the lectern, and a hymn book compiled by a clergyman ot the Church of England was used. Mr. Freeston is a young man, scarcely more than thirty, with a pleasant voice, and he preaches admirably."

In a little pamphlet entitled" Talks about Musi- cians " which has been brought under my notices, there is an appreciative notice of Mr. T. Mee Patti- son. We are told that Mr. Pattison was born in Warrington on January 27th, 1845. "Cantatas and Pat t ison are," we are further informed," substantives so frequently in juxtaposition that the tongue is apt to trip out the two together when it ought not to. Mr. Pattison was a conductor and teacher at War- rington who was hard to please in choosing a work for his choir. He set to work to see if he could not suit their needs himself, and his first essay, The Ancient Mariner," at once made his fame alai proved his gift of melody. Soon composition de- manded his whole time, and he is now eistablisbed in London among the most successful of composers. In an address to young composers, Mr. Pattison once said—" One reason why there wai so muoh uninteresting music was that composers were.. often satisfied with their first ideas. In early life the composer should destroy freely. At Otis period a man considered all his geese to be swanti, but the compot-er's early efforts should be distributed, not by the music publisher, but over the counter of the tallow chandler. Mr. Pattison's motto was: ' • Revise, prune, abbreviate, pat on one side, and re- examine after an interval."

EXPECTED VISIT TO WARRINGTON.

it is stated that the Queen will on the occasion of the formal opdning of the Ship Canal travel through Warrington by the Ls,ndon and North- Western Railway and embark oa the Canal at Latchford, proceeding to the Pomona Dock on the Royal yacht. In order to satisfy himself as to the capacity of the Canal for the safe navigation of the yacht, the Marquis of Lorne on Wednesday went down the waterway. LECTURE ON MASHOYALAND.

On Monday, the Rev. Owen Watkins gave an in- teresting lecture upon " Mashonaland : its ruined cities, ttc.," at the Bold-street Wesh•yan school- room, to a very large audience. After singing and prayer, the Rev. G. D. Mason who presided briefly expressed his pleasure that there was such a good at- tendance that night, and then called upon the lecturer. Mr. Watkins said that the English people had been ac- customed to see the exereme south of Africa coloured red, as belonging to England, but partly by the action of the native chiefs, and partly by the • arrangements with the French, German, and Portugese powers, the a colour had been sot tad over a vdst portion of South Africa. It now extended from Cape Town to the shores of Lake Tangativika, an area equal to the combined areas of Germany, France, Spain, Austria, and Italy, or more than eight thnes,the size of the British Isles. Mate- belelend of which so much had been heard & recently, lay between rivers Limpopo end the Zambezi, and was occunked by the Matabele, an cif-shoot of the once war- like Zulus. It had an area of more than 200,000 equate miles. Matabeleland was an excellent country for colonis- ation, as it was rich in minerals, and also a corn grow- ieg country. The Mao bele people had subjugated the Mashona people, and almost depopulated their country. In Mashonaland, which joined Matabeleland, there were discovered vast ruins of ci en t cities now overgrown with jungle. There was various theories to account for their existence. It was said that they were part of the dominions of the Queen of Sheba.. It is now almost • certain that they were the works of Arabians, certainly prior to the time of the foundation of the Mohammedan faith. The walls of the lareest of these ruined cities were 30 feet high and 30 feetthick, and were anything but jerrybuilt. They were in perfect condition even to this day, and they were held in great reverence by the ' native tribes who lived in that part of the country, who said that they were built by the gods. Mr. Watkins • next went on to give an account of the Matabele people, who were, he said, of a cruel disposition. Referring to Mr. Cecil Rhodes, Mr. Watkins said that he was a per- sonal friend of his, and the natives of South Africa had no better friend than he, and of all the public s,rvants ef the Queen,he was the only one who had never received a penny for his services. A more humane man than he, he never knew in his life. The work ot the South African Company was a blessing to the natives as they prohibited the sale of intoxicating liquors among them. Mr. Watkins concluded his lecture by a general survey of the work done, anti was loudly applauded when he sat down. During the course of the lec.ture,views of the country were thrown on the screen, the lantern being manipulated by Mr. Bodie. A vote of thaeks to the lecturer and Mr. Bcdle, and the pronouncing of the benediction brought the meeting to a close. On Tuesday evening Mr. Watkins repeated the lecture at Padgate wesleyan Chapel before a laree audience.

PEN li Era.

A CHAPTER OF ACCIDENTS.—On Saturday as the 7.39 'bus from Warrington was coming in the village Mr. Picton of Penketh was alighting, but slipped on the step and fell to the ground. He was picked up unconscious and carried into the Red Lion and Dr. Murray was promptly sent for. When he arrived it was found no great damage had been done. Mr. eicton fortunately escaping with a slight cut ou the head and a severe shak- ing. After a short rest h. was able to proceed home.—A., little girl named Walsh was also playing in front of Mr. Longsbaw's stores when a cyclist belonging to a party riding al,;ng collided with her and they were both throws down. The girl's leg was bruised and the rider was severely shaken.—A slight scalding case also happened on Satusiav but it was, happily, not serious. SALM OF FRERHOLI) LAND AND COTTAGII3 Al! .M1:17IILL IMPROYEMIINT ASSOCILTION.—On Wednesday night the weekly meeting was held in the Wesleyan School. The essayist for the. evening should have beeit Mr. T. Gandy, Isot he being U rl/1 bit. to appear A concert was arranged. Mr. Rathhone occupied the chair. Before the commencement of the programme Mr. White, the treasurer, said he was sorry to inform the members that the expenses this year had landed them on the wrong side, for in pact years there had always been a balance in hand Subscriptions were not all paid and perhaps that might bring it up. The Chairman then called en Master and Miss Perks to give their pianoforte duet ! which was, prettily rendered. The next was a piano ! solo, " The March of the Troubadour," by Alas Fair- dough. This was followed by a reading from Jerome, !by Mr. C. E. Parker. A pianoforte solo by Miss Perks ! was followed by a readintr by Mr. Withenshaw. Mr. A. Appleton sang" Tom Bowling," in good style, and the Chairman read- a selection from Longfellow. A piano- forte. solo by Mrs. Parker brought the prlgramme to a I close. A vote of thanks was moved by Mr. Peers and I supported by Mr. T. Bell.

SANKEY' SCHOOL BOARD.

A Board meeting took place on Tuesday after- noon. Major Ridgway, .1.P., presided, and the fol- lowing gent'emen were present :—Mr. W. I'. Skel- ton (vice-chairman), the Rev. E. S. Jackson, and Messrs. Henry Bate and Joseph Wright.—The Treasurer's book showed a balance of £sB4t 31 in favour of the Board.—Cheques were drawn for salaries, &c.—The report of the Master was re- ceived, and showed that the average attendance for the last month had been. 270, and that the number on Ll.t, books was 305.

AN" ACUTE CRISIS."

The political crisis became more acute on Thursday. It is stated that Mr. Gladstone will have an audience of the Queen on Saturday, at which he will tender his re- signation, and that Her Majesty will send for Lord Rose- bery. At the conclusion ot the business in the House of Commons on Thursday,a number of Radicals, headed by Mr. Labouchere and Mr. Storey, waited upon the chief Liberal Whip and intimated that they would object to the Premiership being assumed by a member of the House of Lords.

Tho" Pall Mall Gazette"in h special edition on Thursday night had the following :—" It is definitely settled that Mr. Gladstone will have audience of the Queen on Satur- day,March 3,and will place his resignation in her hands. The public announcement will almost certainly be made on Monday, and Lord Rosebery will be sent for by Her Majesty. There is no present intention of dissolution. The Prime Minister's early resignation was decided up- on as long ago as the opening of the year, but the neces- sary re-arrangements demanded much _consideration. Mr. Gladstone will retain his seat in the House of Com- mons, but will almost cartainly not remain a member of the Cabinet."

ALLEGED BREACH OF AGREEMENT AT RUNCORN.

At the Runcorn County Court on Tuesday. before his lionour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes, an action was heard, in which William Cobley, lodging-house keeper, claimed £5O damages from David Watkin, green grocer, Church-street, for breach of agree- ment, and damage to his premises through the erection of a defective spout. An injunction was aleo asked to restrain the defendant from a continu- ance of a repetition of snch injury. The defendant also made a counter claim for damage done to his land by the erection of a shed, and through hens, Sze., acting as a detriment to himself and to his workmen.—Dr. Thomas, barrister, Liverpool, instructed by Messrs. Davies and Forshaw, solicitors, Warrington, appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. Horridge, barrister, instructed by Messrs. Grace and Smith, of' Liverpool, appeared for ciefenelant.—Dr. Thomas in opening the case for the plaintiff, said that the plaintiff occupied the premisea, 38 Church-street, Runcorn, at present leased to him by the defendant. The lease was dated Feb. 26th,1892, and had been there three years. He was a lodging-house keeper, and also used a horse and trap, as he acted as a horse breaker, which necessitated accommodation for his horse andtrap. In the agreement between the parties it was arranged that the defendant should provide a cart way into Cooper-street, at the rear of the premises, and other things were also agreed upon by Mr. Watkins, and it was in respect of a breach of the agreement, in not pro- viding ingress and egress for the premises that the pre- sent action arose. Mr. Watkins built the stable, and on the plaintiff asking far a road into Cooper-street he was told he could use the one leading into Church-street, which remained open. It was not until Oct. 18th that the cause of the complaint began. On that date, de- fendant began to use the land adjoining Cooper-street, for the purpose of building new cottages. The excava- tions therefrom were placed to a height of six or seven feet so as to prevent plaintiff from getting either into Church-street or Cooper-street. Bricks were also de- posited and a lime pit dug eh the premises, and from Oct. 18th, it was impossible for the plaintiff to get his horse and trap off the premises. On Saturday last however, he received notice that the road into Cooper- street was ready, but the complaint was that they could not get out between Oct. 18th, and last Saturday. As plaintiff had to go to Warrington three or four times a week, owing to the roadway being stopped, he had to go by other means than by thc trap. He had also to send his horse to the farm of Mr. Whitlow. He was also un- able to carry on his business as horse-breaker, and thus, lost considerable profit. Another portion of the claim was that a shop had been erected close to the premises of plaintiff down the side of which had been placed a , spout. This was unfinished, no mouth piece being con- nected with it, and the water from the roof descended upon Cobley's land. As there was a gradient of one in twelve towards the lodging house, the water flowed into his cellar, and at times accummulated to the depth of a foot. Although he had complained, he could get no re- dress. The damage claimed was £lO for this, and also asked for an injunction that defendant be restrained from dredging the water in future. The plaintiff had received a counterclaim on the ground that Mr. Watkins had suffered damage by trespass, by erecting a shed, by keeping on the land a number of fowls and bricks, plac- ing thereon a dog kennel,and having a ferocious dog. The , plaintiff asserted that the sheds were on the land at the , time if the lease, and had not been erected by him, and they muet have been there to the knowledge of the defen- dant when the agreement was entered into. William Cobley gave evidence confirming the learned council's stet, moot.

Leonard Ne _vcombe, architect, Penketh, gave evidence of the water g• tting into the cellar of the plaintiff's. In crss-examination, he said that it might have got in through bad drains. James Percival, inspector of nuisances for the Runcorn Rural Sanitary Authority, said that he could not get the horse and cart to the house in November to clear away the rubbish, owing to the amount of mortar and bricks which were in the way. The men had to get planks and wheel the stuff away with barrows for which they ciarged Mr. Cobley extra. William Hickman, auctioneer, Warrington, said that he I requently visit.d the houso of the plaintiff. He had haa to go over the shoe tops in water to get to the house in wet weather.

George Duckett, Church-street, fishmonger, said he sold the premises in questien to Mr. Cobley. There was a shed on the ground at the time, which he had built in 1887.

Henry Eaton, foreman under the inspector of nuis- ances, gave corroborative evidence to the witness Percival.

William Wright, Church-street, and Robert Ellison also gave evidenco to the way to the house being blocked by the defendant's building material. Henry Whitlow, farmer, Halton Gate, said that he received a horse from the plaintiff on October 18, to keep for him as he could not use it. Plaintiff agreed to pay 10s. a week and he kept it 12 weeks. He had not been paid the £6 yet. At this stage the court adjourned for the day.

A BILLIARD EXHIBITION AT RUNCORN.

One of the most interesting exhibitions of• bil- liards ever seen in Runcorn was witnessed. on Mon- day evening at Mr. Shone's, George and Dragon Hotel, Egerton-st. The combatants were the well- known Manchester player, E. Diggle, and T. Ryder, of Liverpool. Mr. Diggle holds the championship of the north, and his recent exhibitions, especially against Roberts, has made his name familiar to all "billiardists" throughout the country, his victory last week ever Mit- chell, of London, also adding considerably to his reputa- tion. Ryder holds the championship of Liverpool, and that his abilities are not much inferior to those of his opponent was proved by the result of the exhibition. As may be imagined, there was a very large audience to witness the play. It was originally intended that a game of 2,000 up should be played, Ryder to have 200 points conceded to him. Tnrough the Mancheater man missing his train he put in a late appearance, and consequently two games at 500 each were arranged, the Liverpudlian receiving a start of 100 in each contest. Ryder was the I first to commence play, and he, led off with a break of 41. Diggle quickly compiled 63, in which some of the shots were difficult ones. The same player followed with breaks of 41 and 31, whilst his opponent raised his num- bers somewhat slowly, h.s best register for some time being 41. The northern champion subsequently made rapid advance, his chief contributions being 137, 27,and 60. The first named break proved to be the best effort Of the evening, and gained for the player rounds of. ap- plause. Ryder put together 72,the principal features of which were a number of nursery cannons, in which he delights. Diggle ran out the winner by 500 to 419. The next game was "spot barred." Diggle began with a break of 52, compiled in rare style,and his oppo- nent only succeeded in putting together 26. Diggle afterwards increased his figures by 56. The next not- able feature was one in which the Liverpudlian made a break of 90, and in this effort also his skill was manifest in nursery cannons. Diggle's subsequent scores were 31. 69, and 23, and the principal efforts of Ryder were 'a splendidly compiled 51 and 20. After this the game became most interesting and exciting,the con- testants rept atedly passing and repassing each other. Ryder showed excellency of form, ,and increased his figures by 73, this making his score 458. Digale replied with 76, by which he again drew several points in ad- vance of his antagonist. Missing a somewhat easy shot let in Ryder, who advanced speedily to 496, when he experienced the hardest of luck. Diggle followed and ran out, thas winning the game by 500 to 496. At the close of the game Mr. Diggle expressed his admiration of the table,which had been used that night for the first time, and which was manufactured by Mr. S. Sowerby, of Liverpool.

THE CHURCH INSTIT UTE, 1100ItE.

At the village of Moore, near Warrington, on Sunday, the wooden building to be known as the Chive) Institution, the gift of Canon Heron, was formerly opened. The structure was formerly the offices of the Ship Canal Company at Weston Point but was taken down and transplanted to Moore, where it has been transformed into an edifice of no pretentioua appearance, but eminently suited for the purposes for which it is intended. The Dean of Chester and Mr. Wynne Jones were among the speakers. and in the course of speeches At the open- ing of the proceedings, and subsequent to the ad- dresses of other gentlemen, Canon Heron, now in his eighty-sixth year, expressed his pleasure at the great appreciation by which his gift had been received. The present building was provided by a Churchman, and, as far as a forecast could be made, in the future its maintenance would mainly depend upon members of the Church; but as regarded the benefits ot the institu- tion these would be open to everybody wishing to take advantage of them. He wished to say a few words on the drink question. It Was a most difficult question, °but he had never been able to reconcile himself to the demands of the teetotallers. If a person felt inclined t) give way, as a Christian man he was lx,und to keep away from drink (" Hear, hear," from the Dean of Ches- ter), but as for persons who could reasonably draw distinction between the use and the abuse of a thing, he had never been able to arrive at the conclusion to deny a workingman the restorative pleasure and benefit of a glass of good malt liquor after his day's work. Wine and spirits were excluded as regards the material recrea- tions in Moore Church Institution, but a person had undertaken to come each evening to ask if any gentle- man in the institution wished to have a glass of beer. This person would take in the number of pints on receiv- ing the money, and thohe wishing could thus get their beer. If there arose any abuse of this privilege—and he hoped the committee would inform him 4f any abuse did arise—he would close the institution. This method, he thought, would satisfy a man of ordinary intelligence it was a middle way between total abstinence and tem- perance.

DEATH OF SERGEANT-MAJOR

KE.A.RNEY.

A CRIMEAN HERO. MILITARY FUNERAL. It is with regret that we have to chronicle this week the sudden death of Mr. W. R. Kearney, of Rirley-st.. Newton, late Sergeant-Major of the flth V.B.K.L.R. (Press Guards), which took place at his late residence at neon on Friday, the 23rd ult., at the age of WS years. Mr. Kearney was employed at Messrs. McCerquodale and Co.'s works, Newton, aud on Friday morning vraa in his usual health and spirits. He returned to dinner abour 1.30, and was suddenly seised when reaching home, with an apoplectic ftt. Dr. Wat- kins was hastily summoned, but the deceased never recovered consciousness, and died about 5.30 in the evening. He leaves a widow but no family. Deceased has been connected with the Liverpool Press Guards fer upwards of 20 years as sergeant-major, but owing to failing health he resigned about two years as, and re- moved to Newton. His service: ia the regiment were very highly appreciated by both officers and men, and he thoroughly understood all the duties attached to his position. He was of a kindly a gentlemanly demeanour, but a strict disciplinarian. As Sergeant Major he was much esteemed, and when in mainland of the regiment, every member of it had the fullest confidence in his ability, and were pleased to be under his care. Not only had he done good service with the Volunteers, but his connect-jots with the regular forces was of a meritorious character, proof being found in the fact that he was the possessor of six service medals. He was a native of Dublin, and at the age ef 16 years, joined the 34th regiment of foot. At the outbreak of the Crimean war, his regiment was drafted there, and he fought through the whole of the campaign, and al- though he was many times present at furious onslaughts, ' and at one time one of a forlorn hope, he came out with- vat a scratch. He was present at the storming and cap- ture of the Medan, and also of the storming of Sebasto- , pool, and for his services during the war he received the medal and clasp for Sebastopool, French war medal, Turkish and Sardinian medal, and a medal for distin- guished conduct in the field, four of which only were [given to each regiment. At the doge of the Crimean we r he applied for furlough, but before it could he granted, his regiment was drafted to India at the outbreak of the Indian Mutiny, and served with much distinction with the relieving force. He was at the siege of Lucknow and for this he received the medal and clasp " Lucknow 1857 and 52." He used to relate that at one time he and sevetal others had to make a dash across a plain to a place of safety, under a hot fire from the enemy,and, though several of them were were shot down,he and two or three of his comrades suc- ceeding in reaching the home lines unscathed. He was however during the campaign shot in the leg, and up to the time of his death, the effects of the wound ca used him to limp slightly when ws lking. He was also awarded the medal for distinction and long service.

===== 0002601_18940303_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
TIIII FUNERAL.

The body was interred with full military honours on Tuesday afternoon in cemetery of the Catholic Church, Newton, deceased having been an ardent catholic. The funeral cortege left Birley-street about 4 c'clock, pro- ceeded by the band of the 6th V.8.K.L.R., from Liver- pool, and the local bugle band, the former playing the "Dead March." A firing party with rifles reversed, consisting of 18 members of the regiment, followed. Then came the , hearse containing the collie on which vies the regimental flag, deceased's helmet, and sword, and a number of wreaths. • A mourning coach containing the relatives and immediate friends of the deceased, and was fol- lowed by Colonel Wood, Colonel Whitney, late of the 6th V. 8., Major Pride, Sergeant-Instructer Sweeney, and •, other officers, and about 50 members from the regiment from Liverpool, and 30 of the Newton Company. Several officers of the sth L.R.V. were also present, anti about 15 non-commissioned officers and men of the Newton Company of the Ist P.W.S L. R.,including Drill Instruc- tor Wooding and Sergeant-Major Sullivan of Warrington the rear being brought up by a detachment of the Hussars under Sergeant Major Strange, and a number of civilians, friends of the deceased. A large number of people lined the route, and the Newton tradespeople closed their places of business during the passage ef the procession. The service at the church was inapreeeively conducted by the Rev. F. J. Lennon, and at the close the band played a funeral hymn. 'rho coffin was after- wards borne to the grave by six members of the regi- ment, and at the conclusion of the service at the grave side, a " feu de jeu " was fired over the grave, the cere- mony being wound up by the bugle band sounding the call unusally given on such occasions. •

ST lIELENS LIBERAL ASSOCIATION.

THE REPRESENTATION OF THE BOROUGH. The annual meeting of the St. Helens Liberal Association was held at the St. Helens Town flail, on Wednesday night. In the absence of Alderman Cook, president of the association, Councillor John Forster, 'chairman of the Executive Committee presided. Amongst those present were Councillors J. Burchaq, J. P. learns, J. B. Leach, 11. Martin, F. A. Burt, J. Jones, J. A. Chadwick and J. Collins ; Messrs. H. A. Clarlw, P. Dromgoole, 0. Gregson, T. It Chester- ton, A. Griffin, W. Morris, A. Murray, J. Rimmer, W. H. Perkins, W. . Green, Stephenson,- T. Roberts, G. Hewitt, sen. Murray,;G. Hewitt, jun., W. Burrows, A. Twist, A. Francis, R. Marsh, W. Fletcher, Sedsion,W Seath, M. Phillips, .J. Povey, P. Murphy, D. Chadwick, A. E. Edwards, P. Grundy, J. H. Houghton, and Mr. J. Fain (Liberal agent). ' The Chairman, in briefly reviewing the positioo of the Liberal party, said, as regarded the local situation, he ventured to express the opinion—they could take it for what it was worth—that the pesition of the Liberal party in St. Helens was distinctly improved to what it was at the time of the election. They had there that night, and within call, not only all the men they had at the last election, who were ready to stand by their can- didate whenever they had him before the constituency —they had not only men determined to work as hard as before, or even harder, but they had a number of men who at the last election were either listless or were on the other side—(hear, hear.)—men who ha l seen the hollowness of the principles of their present representa- tive, who, in a word, had gulled them. He (the chair- man) had heard of, not one man, but dozens of men, who had worked on the Conservative side in 1835, 1836, and 1892, who said they would try what they could do, by putting their shoulders to the wheel, to make an improvement in the representation of St. Helens. (applause.) Councillor Martin (treasurer) reported that the s ssoci- abort was in a better financial position than ever before. If the party had the misfortune to lose Mr. Gladstone's brilliant services, they had still men of tact and ability —Lord Roseberry, who settled the miners' dispute so satisfactorily ; and Sir William Harcourt, whose speech at Portsmouth showed that he was becoming even more brilliant in his political career than before. They had no occasi, n to feel faint-hearted about the prospects of the Liberal party. (Applause.) Councillor J.B.Leach moved a vote of thanks to 'Alder- man Cook for his services as president during the past year, and proposed his re-election. Councillor j. Alea.rns seconded, and the resolution was carried unanimously. Councillor Martin was re-elected treasurer, an Conn- ' culler J. P. Alearns hon. see. The Chairman then said the most important business of the meeting was to consider the Liberal candidature of the borough. The object of the association had been to give everybody allied to Liberalism a voice in the choice of a candidate. (Applause.) The Executive Committee had to submit to the meeting the following resolution :—" That this meeting recommends the mem- bers of the St.-Helens Liberal Association at their annual meeting to ask Mr. William Woods, J.P., of Wigan, to become the Liberal candidate for the . borough of St. Helens." Mr. Ga,skill moved the resolution, and said that if Mr. Woods was anything like his father, he was a strong, robust Liberal. If he had turned a little back and be- come a Conservative, he had again taken up his true position. Mr. J. H. Houghton seconded the resolution, which was supported by Mr. W. Morris, Mr. W. Rimmer, Mr. T. 11. Chesterton, Mr. B. Marsh, Mr. W. H. Perkins,Mr. Green, and Mr. Stevenson. The Chairman said he believed they would have in Mr. Woods a candidate who was a thorough-going Radi- cal—a man who went further than the Newcastle pro- gramme in Isis desire for measures for the good of the working classes everywhere, a gentleman who liberally ssisted the working classes from his pocket when they had most needed help, and one whose amiability and frankness commended itself to everybody who had met him at the Volunteer Hall. The resolution was then carried unanimously. Councillor Jas. Burchall then proposed the following resolution :—" That the habitual disregard of the national will manifested by the House of Lords, in de- laying, mutilating, and rejecting measures demanded by the country and approved of by the House of Commons, is an intolerable abuse of the powers possessed by the hereditary and non-representative chamber ; and that this meeting earnestly hopes that measures will be adopted by the .Government to secure that the House of Commons shall be the paramount authority in the , State." , Mr. R. Marsh seconded,and the resolution having been supported by Mr. Daft, it was carried unanimously. , A vote el'. thanks to the Chairman concluded the pro- ceedings.

Dr. T. de Witt Talmage refuses to reconsider his resig- nation. and announces his intention to preach his fare- well sermon at the Brooklyn Tabernacle on the first Sun- day in March, and on April Ist to start with his wife and daughters on a trip round the world. The party is to be absent several months, and will visit California, New Zealand, Australia, and other places.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. An article by Mr. Vhitharn, and letter from John Peers it held over.

RUNCORN SCHOOL, BOARD.

Sir,—Will some of the members of the Board explain how it i 3 that they are only paying female pupil leachers the enormous sum of thirty-seven pounds for four years service? I consider it a disgraae to the town to offer or expect people to allow their children t) slave tor such a paltry sum. The Liverpool School Board are offering sixty-eigLt pounds and ninety-three pounds for female and male pupil teachers respectively. The Runcorn Board, I consider, are not doing fairly to their servants ; ths labourer is worthy of lair pay, and if the youths are not treated so, they may expect to have trouble with them the same as at Widnes. The town is mismanaged in this and ether things. and I hdl not be surprised to see many more empty houses in Runcorn before another yell' is over. I hope the Board will ;ict as gantlernen and do as they wenilcl he done by. Don't grind work (mental) from your tlervantswhist you are starving them.—Yours, &c., February 21, 1824.

RUNCORNIAN.

THE SOUL OF THE SEED.

Sir,—Onee again I must trouble you in order te *tear away the obscurity and muddle which Mr. Peers it's I created around my short letter on the above subject. I I did not say nor infer that seed had " conscious life" so our frieed's respect for vegetables in eeneral will ne doubt decline. The •riq,inal question was aimed at line Divinity of Christ—tne means taken to show Christ's ignorance—erye falliole humanity, was the parable of the seed. Ii therefore Christ said that the seed " died " and science now proves it does not die, Christ of course thought Mr, reers—nsust be—well—very ignorant, and anything else you like. I then showed that what Christ meant, when he said "the seed dies," and an Atheist's meaning of the tsrm (including Mr. Peers) were diaime- txically opp iced to each other. The former intended to show that the seed underwent a total change in the -aertk, before it could rise as an ear 0: wheat, and used this as an analezy. Mr. Peers and all Atheists, when they use the words " die" or" death "mean the total extinction of all life. Science tells us that nothing what ever in nature dies in the above sense, and reigion tells ne that man is no exception to this natural law, or as the old 13.oman expressed it 2500 years ago" non cmnis utoriar," " 1 all not all die." This plain statement, may perhaps show the dishonest and illogieal methods used, to try and score a point against-Christienity.—Yours faithfully, JOHN S. HOWELL.

===== 0002601_18940303_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DEAD-SEED THEORY.

Sir,—Our friend " Ben Chayil," does not waist to waste his time, and yet he writes a long letter, ropes t- ing the arguments he last used ,and saying nothing new. Last time he wrote, so little real matter had he to use that he wallowed in epithets, and last week he again wade use of language, which was neither becoming to himself, nor manly towards his oppment. Among Other choice words we have : "cavillers," " pedants," " ignorance," " insolence,"" impudent slander,"" idle jargon," " word-mongering," " prosumptious foot." *6 Ben "appears to want to pose as a scientist, and as far removed from a " local illirnitatus," and this is the languaee he has to fall back upon. For myself, I must believe that the seed does not die, and even Benjamin himself owns it doesn't, for it is a mere quibble to say it does, because forsooth, the out- aide shell cannot be said to live. The Greeks,Egyptians, Romans, and Jews are quoted as having believed in the seed dying, but wonderful and admirable as were these nations, they were saturated with superstition, and are by no means to be relied en in such a matter as this. All these people believed in oracles, yet waiknow they were mistaken. Ilerodotus says the sun is blown about by winter storms, yet he was mistaken ; and many things were firmly believed in by these grand nations that would not stand the light to &y, and" they didn't know everything down in Judee." Moreover, an as- sertion so sweeping as this needs proof. But as" Ben" himself says that the embryo does not die, I cannot see what need there is for further discussion; the point disputed by Harry Esmond is conceded, and there is an end of the matter.—Yours,

SNODGRLSS.

ItONOUR TO WHOM HONOUR IS DUN. Sir,—l was one of those who constituted the large audience present at the meeting on Friday night, held in connection with the Temperance Federation, when we bad for our Chairman, Councillor H. Roberts. The speeches on the whole were very edifying, inspiriting, and entertaining, and I trust they will be the means of increasing the good work the Terepera.nce Federation is doing and has done for a long time. The only remark made with which I don't agree, came from the lips of the chairraan, and was to the effect that the banal press "bad not done its duty by the Temperance party and bad been for some time past apathetic, neutral, and hostile t* the best interests of the town generally." The term "apathetic " is meant, I presume, to apply to the "Examiner." Now Sir, I have read and watched the " Examiner " carefully for a number of years, more particelarly from the time the late Mr. Walker became the editor and proprietor, and I maintain that if ever a man and a newspaper worked hard for the priaciplea of temperance, " and the best interests of the tows generally," that man was Mr. Walker, and that newspaper, the Warrington "Examiner," and I have certainly not noticed that the same principles are not upheld and not worked for now as they were then. Mr. Roberts speaks occasionally for Liberalism and Temper- ance, but a weekly newspaper speaks once a week all the year round. I have great respect for Mr. Roberts, but I don't approve of everything he does or *wen ElNys ; he is no more perfect, than the rest of us,but sometimes lets his tongue get the better of his head, which makes him say things that were better unsaid. Take for instance his ungentlemanly and uncalled-for attack on a brother Councillor at the last Council meeting,in a remark which, for once, he had to withdrar.—Yours sincerely, TEETOTALER.

Now, as to my perplexity, I think I made it pretty clear in my first letter that I believed the seed (lid not die. Although I had at one time bean perplexed about . this I was not at the time of writinz. My real perplexity was in understanding how the "Methodist Recorder" \ad Professor Davidson reconcile their strange theories about the limitations of Christ's knowledge with the doctrine of His Divinity. I was satisfivd myself that in the case of the seed His knowledge was limited,but how to reconcile this limitation with His Divinity—well, as I aiid before,that's the rub. And because I seek enlighten- ment "Ben Chayil" has poured a torrent of abuse upon we. I wonder if his Divine Master would have done $o

conclusion,may I ask "Ben Chavil" two questions ? In which way is this statement 'if -Christ's about the Seed dying, " a beautiful and apt illustration of His Divine prescience and wisdom." and does he believe thp " Methodist Recorder " and Professor Davidson to be animated with a malignant spirit of hostility towards religion in advancing those theories Sbout the [imitations of Christ's knowledge ?—Yours, HARRY ESMOND.

"BEN CHAYIL" ON VEGETABLE PHYSIOLOGY. Prove all tl4inas ; hold fast that which is good." " Facts are chieli that winir,' ding."

Sir,—! entertain a sort of feelieg of half amused corn enieeration for poor Ben. From his own showing lie " smells a rat :" he thinke, and perlieps rightly, that " Harry " has been "drawing him out," and is natur- ally " mad " that he should have been so easily lured. But will he allow me to suggest that a mind so easily thrown off its balance is not ',ell adapted to weighing the elements of a scientific problem. Mere rant is detri- mental alike to the user and to the cause it is used to sustain. Had poor "Ben " hut spent some of the time wasted in collecting opprobrious epithets to pelt his op- ponents.in the better way of collecting facts and observ- ing Nature, he might easily have confuted his own fallacy and that of his traditional authority. But like the multitude, he finds it easier to rant than to ob- serve and demonstrate. He displays an utter inability in stating the question at issue not only sciontifically,but in ordinary language. His crudities in elementary bot- any are all the more noticeable in one So tree with the charge of eciolisin against others. Let us bring him to the bar for cross examination What, for instance, is meant—" by the verisimilitude to the laws of decay and death of the investiture, the ex- ternal wrappings and integuments of the cornseed—"? Here we have six most unfortunate words for poor Ben! As to the verisimilitude—" Ben " has by his ignorance of the actual facts, been deceived by an appearance then as to the "decay and death" el investiture, the exter- nal wrappings and integuments (sic)—perhaps he will tell a " mere sciolist" what this curious jumble means ? As to the "decay and death "of the pericarp any wheat held just now will furnish forth a full confutation of the fallacy of the" decay and death" of the integument. It is true that the embryo has by a proms of growth,not of death, emptied the perica.rp of its farinaceous albu- men but we cannot use such words as "decay and . death" to describe its condition. Indeed the whole ques- tion turns upon the definition of vegetable life. Can "Ben Chayil " frame a definition which shall cover his conten- tion ? If so, the thing is settled. But, judging from his natural history acquirements, it would be cruelty to ask him for one. The text says it is " not quickened, except it die." "It" here can only mean the embryo and that does " quicken" without any part of the grain dy- ing " decaying " or undergoing any moribund " Finally let Mr." en"B continue his researches and give more time be the study of cell-structure. and I will, on one condition, promise within twelve calendar months, to put both himself and his Sunday school authority to the blush. The condition I attach is that he be asked for an explanation, over his own name, within that time. The initiated will then know who they are laughing at. Truly yours,

JOHN PEERS.

Sir,—Perhaps. if your correspondent Mr. Flowcroft, cares to continue this discussion on Socialism, it would be as well if he defined what he means by selfishness. which he alleges is the " active principle "of Socialism. And here let me observe at the outset that when we have obtained the Socialist goal we do not say we shall have- arrived at perfection. Our present system is the apoth nosis of selfishness, and Socialism is a great improve- ment upon it. As things are now, the great army of workers do the sowing, and the rAaping, and others come along and seize the crop. Under Socialism the sewer and the reaper would have the crop, and the other fellow would have to whistle for the wind. Socialism, says our critic, gains her converts by ap- pealing to the cupidity of those" who have not." So far the number of such converts has been small, and that is the disappointing feature of the.movement ; the converts, the most active and zealous of them, are from the ranks of those who have. It exasperates me to rind that those whom Socialism will benefit most are so backward in giving their support to it. It is practicallv only a question of votes, and vet these men, with splen- did unselfishness g. on voting for the party of privilege.

wealth and property! But just let us examine for a moment Mr. Flowcroft's argument o: selfishness, though it is somewhat remark- able that this quality which forms the basis of society as at present constituted, should be quoted in condem- nation of the coming system. Could we ask for a more scathing denunciation. of the present ? But the areu- agent is that selfishness is at the bettom of the movement, because it appeals to those who have not. " Noble spinners, this is the one hundred thousand we have gained, wherein I mean to dwell and plant vineyards. The hundred thousand is wine, the three and sixpence daily is yours ; drink my heelth with this groat each, which I give you over and above." This man of the one hundred thousand belongs to t'se "haves," the noble spinners of the throe and sixpence daily are the" have nots," and because Sscialisce would alter this rernerk- able method of distribution, in other words, would give the noble spinner his just due, selfishness is declared to be at t,,e bottom of it! Can it he that Mr. Howcroft is unable to distinguish ketween selfishness and a desire to have what one is entitled to, no more and no less? No, Socialism is not selfish, but it is sternly just. Appealing to the cupidity of those who have not! Why have they not ? Did it ever occur to Mr. Howeroft to ask himself that qoesstioa ? Our critic in his defence of individualism, in his defence of a system which is in direct contradiction to the teach- ings of Christ,appeals to the cupidity of those who have. Appeal to these peeple, he says, and they won't accept Socialism. Many of them probably will not, of their own free will and choice. Why P Because they believe the community is made for them and not they for the community. But they are wrong. We must have gov- ernment of the people by the people for the good of the people, and not fur the material good of the few. Thera is danger in great fortunes, not only to those who possess them, but to the people at large. It is not merely from an E s. d. point of view that the enlielitened of all classes must regard Socialism. There are higher ideals than that of mere money-mak-hie. As things are now, a man's whole life is spent in an endeavour to keep body and soul together. Everything ehet is sacrificed to this ; his better nature is blunted and degraded ; any aspirations to a higher and nobler life are crushed; because the time and the wealth lie , creates are applied not for the pu I pese of his own uplirt- ing, hut for prov:ding hurtful luxuries for the wealthy and for the maintenance of idlers, rich and poor alike. But our friend of Middlesbro' is troubled with such thiges as the p,.euniary queltinn--"economical manufac- ture." He thinks tee ceannunit e would net be able to make things as cheaply as a private individual. Some people I know think that the object of life le to maks tood and furniture and not life itself, but our practical friend from Yorkshire evidently believes it to be to manufacture economically. We are a wonderful people. Might it not, as Ruskin says, pay as well to turn out healthy men and women and manufacture human sowis ? The acme of human perfection is attained when the worker by an almost miraculous dexterity can polish so many hundred parts of an article in an hour. And he goes on doing this hour after hour, day after day, year after year,until his heart is :is hard as the iron or his head as wooden as the article that he so deftly manipulates. We are indeed a wonderful people. While he is doing this, the other chaps—l mean the men who delft do this —are having a gay old time of it with the wealth they have made by the sleight of hand performances of the aforesaid automaton. And the other chaps leave their sons the " one hundred thousand which is mine," and they go and do likewise, and frequently gain an unde- served reputation for being men of ability. So the wonderful automaton has had kiis three and sixpence— not has it, mind you, but has had it—and what more does he want? And don't you come, you Socialist agita- tor, appealing to his cupidity; it won't do to have his cupidity disturbed when he is engaged in dexterous manceuvres, because it would interfere with economical' manufacture for which be was designed. Can it he ? No—but for which he is certainly utilized and taught to believe in as the be-all and the end-all of life. We are indeed a ?nest noble race ! What is the cause of this determination to manufac- ture economically ' • this fervent desire to make thiegs cheaper and shoddier than anybody else ? Competition. But the state or the municipality becoming the manu- facturer would not in a national sense be subject to com- petition. In what way then should we be injured ? Our railway companies are co-operative or socialistic; so are all companies in varying degrees,anu is not the tendency more and more towards combination? We light our streets and houses, we supply ourselves with water and conduct our sanitary arrangements on Socialistic lines, and the State carries our lettere and forwards cur tele- grams for us. And since under Socialism we ehoild have no unemployed to keep,there would be no difficulty in holding our own. I ehould not have referred to the condition of the worker a few centuries back,except that Mr. Howcroft asked if people were not as a whole better off than ever they were. I quoted Hallam in my former letter, but the late Professor Therold Rogers proves that the worker of the middle ages was many times better off than his fellows of to-day. Mr. William Morris has paid rime attention to this subject, and he said, so re- cently: as last Monday, that "the workman of the . middle ages was better fed, better clothed, and better

THE TOWN CLOCK.

- Sir.—" Elickary Dickory " apparently doubts the ac- curacy of my statements about the town clock. He asks how the Birkenhead gun-time was obtained in Warrinr- ton, and now the error of a second hand can be.detected .to 1 sec. The latter question is answered in my first let- ter, where I expressly stated that my figures represented the errors of the clock in striking the hour ; and I may add that I experience no ddEculty in noting the time of striking to sec. with the aid of ray watch. But another question may be raised here. It may be asked, is the strike of the clock sufficiently precise to indicate exactly its variations from week to week ? Practically it is. The first stroke of 12 o'clook does not as a rule differ from that of 3,6, or 9, by so much as 1 sec. when the clock is keeping unvarying time and the hours of 2,5, 8, and 11, are equally precise, but 21 secs. later. This is the greatest variation in the strike between any twa hours of the same day ; and being systematically regu- lar, it can be allowed for, if observations cannot always be made at the same hour or one of the series of four to which it belongs. The Birkenhead gun-time was not obtained in War- rington, but on the landing stage at Livcrpoohand care- fully noted to a fraction of a second by means of a watch which rarely varies more than a second in 24 boors. " Hickory Dickory " also asks "if such a clock as that at Trinity should keep time within 1 sec. per week." Theoretically it should, if the pendulum is per- fectly compensated for changes of temperature ; but owing to the uncertainty of the zinc and iron compensa- tion used in turret clocks, thia condition is not always attained. I have had abundant evidence of the trutk of" Hic- kory Dickory's concluding suggestions ; and it is solely with a view to remove this unreasonable distrust of the clock that I have occasionally written letters about it to the local papers. If there be any still sceptical as to its reliability, I wish they would compare it occasionally with the clock (a regulPtor), at the entrance to Messrs. Russell's stunt) in San key-st., noting the exact second when it strikes.—Yours, P.W.R.

- - Sir.—After reading "Bea Chayil's " latest effusion I am more perplexed than ever.
