The minds of philosophers and others were struck with the seeming ovidencAs of the de- cay and death of the seed, but the detection of a minute portion of the seed in which lay the life germ was to be the heritige of future days." Now if the statement is correct, as I believe it to be, why on earth should he trot out Dr. Balfour, or any other writer of primers, to prove that the see.] does die? Does not " Ben Chayil " cast reflections on Chriets' knowledge when he makes this statement, as well as the" Metho- dist Recorder "and my-elf? And is not tbo charge of being animated with "a malignant spirit of hostility towards religion" just as applicable to him ? To say that the statements cf Christ and St. P. el about the seed dying does not imply the death of the germ, or embryo S,as I said before, the merest quibbling.

ACTION BY WAILRINGTON TRADEIE.I.. On Wednesday Mr. Ju,tice Kekewich heard the motion of Messrs. of rarliament) so as to injure er obstruct the plaintiff's rights to navigate the said river between their land, ' wharves, • works, and mills, known as the Mersey Mills, situate on the bank of the river at Warrington, and the . estuary .•,f the river ; or so 9.3 to injure, interfere with, or • obstruct the plaintiffs' right to use the water of the river for power to work their mills. and from carrying on dredging or any other work or operations in such a manner as to cause the silting up ot the river or obstruc- tion to the navigation thereof between the points afore- raid. Mr. Warmington, Q.C., and Mr. Hughes appeared for the plaintiffs ; Mr. F. Moniton, Q.C., and Mr. Sebes-

tian appeared for the defendant Company. Mr. Warrningtan said he did not intend to proceed with the second half of the motion relating to dredging, Scc., in respect of which there was some conflict. Briefly, what was asked for was to grant an injunction to re- strain the Canal Company from taking more water from the river Mersey than they were entitled to under their Acts of Parliament. The learned counsel then produced two plans showing the course of the river and the points of intake and the points at which Howley Quay and Howley Weir were situate. He then proceeded to refer to the sections of the Act of 1885, showing the restric- tions imposed on the Company with regard to the intake of water from the river at Howley Quay, which was limited to ten million of gallons in 24 hours when the flow past that point was 100 million of gallons in the 24 hours, and also showing the provision made for the con- struction and maintenance of water gauges. The learned counsel then proceeded to read the affidavit of the plain- tiffs, which was to the effect that at no time since the opening of the Canal. had 100 million of gallons of water passed the point indicated. Mr. Sebastian then read the affidavit of Mr. Leader Williams, engineer to the Canal Company, which stated that for some -weeks pact the Crompany had been in nego- tiation with the Corporation of Warrington upon 'zhe subject of the construction of the gauges required to be made. hut up to the present no agreement had been come to. Until the designs of the gauges had been settled by agreement or arbitration it was impossible for the Com- pany to carry out the provisions of the Act. Until these works had been carried out no reliable evidence could be obtained with reference to the quantity of water that flowed down the Mersey. The calculations made by the other side were, he believed, incorrect. Deponent sub- mitted that it was merely a question of what should be done under the provisions of the Act, and this should be determined by arbitration. Mr. Warmington pointed nut that in the schedule at- tached to the affidavit of his engineer she results of the observations were carefully given. The affidavit also stated that at the time when the mills were working it did not mean that they were working full time, because frequently the head of water was not sufficient to grind the full weight of corn. As to the construction of gauges, they would not give the quantity of water ab- stracted. The introduction to section 12 stated that it was for the protection of the Corporation of Warrington and of the traders, manufacturers, and ethers carrying on business at or near Warrineton. Until this Act waspas sad the plaintiffs' millewere fed by thewateranove,which for some distance was in the township of Warrington. , Mr. Moultoe said that this was a question, in fact, of a mandatory injunction. The defendants received the. whole of the waters of the canal, but they had to restore I a certain amount to the Mersey so that it went along by the plaintiffs mills. All he could say was that the evide, ence that the defendants did net do their duty was not sufficient te, show that they did not ; but he was not there to suggest that they did not want to do it. They were willing to take all reasonable steps as quickly as they could to ascertain what [key were doing and tto- take care that they did what the Act of Parliament put upon them. And it appeared that what the Act put upon them was that they should allow to flow down the stream of the old Mersey such a portion of water as was specified under sub-section 12 of the Act of 1888. They had to turn out of the canal, which had to receive the whole of the waters of the Mersey, the proportion referred to in section 12. The quantity of water in cubic feet flowing past Howley Quay meant the actual amount flowing, arid if the actual amount was such and such the Com Dane, were permitted totake ten millions. Mr. Justice Kekewich: What, in the next 24 hours ? Mr. Moulton : In the same 24 hours. Mr. Justice Kekewich : That is absolute nonsense. If the water flowing down is 100 millions,' and you are allowed to abstract tee millions, then it is not 100 mil- lions. I confess Ido not understani the section. Mr. Moulton said he was not responsible for the Act of Parliament, hut it certainly appeared contradictory. , Mr. Justice Kekewich said Mr. Warmiegton's inter- pretation was—if, 'int for the operations of the Canal Company, there would in any 24 hours be a quantity not exceeding 100 millions, then the operations of the Com- pany would be lawful if they diminished that t 3 the extent of ten millions. Mr. Moultoe : I tan% think that can be. Mr. Justice Kekewich : Ido not say that that la the censtruction of the words, but it may be the meaning of them. Mr. Moulton : I think the quantity of water must be measured by what actually passes Howley Quay in 24 hours. Mr. Justice Kekewich : It is quite impossible to dimin- ish that which is actually there. Mr. Moulton : That ie clear. I suppose after the 24 hours, they could say : "You diverted 15 millions; therefore that is a breach of your statutory duty, be- cause you exceeded the actual quantity." Mr. Moulton went on to say that he quite felt his Lordship's diffi- culty, and he would be perfectly willing that some en- gineer on behalf of the plaintiffs and another en behalf of the Company should meet like reasonable people and see what was best to be doneeend ascertain whether the Company were doing their duty and what they could do. He wished to say on behalf of the Canal Company that they desired to be reasonable. Mr. Moulton ; That is a matter which I will not trouble myself about now. This question might stand over so that we could ascertain what has been done. Of course, in their evidence, there is nothing to show what has gone on for 21 hours. Mr. Justice Kekewich : I don't know about that. Mr. Warmington failed to see the good of accepting Mr. Moulton's offer. After further argument, Mr. Moulton said he must leave it to his Lordship to construe the section. Mr. Justice Kekewich : I have great hesitation in do- ing so, because the section is certainly not expressed in clear language. Ido not know whether I may venture to say that it is not expressed according to the ordinary rules of the grammar of the English language. But it is not only the difficulty of the section to construe, but there is also the fact that it is one section of a vast scheme of the Legislature, all of which ought to be con- sidered. After some further criticism of the wording of the Act, his Lordship said : I think I must put a somewhat strained construction upon it in order to give it sense. I think it must mean this—that if but for the interfer- ence of the canal works there would be in any 24- hours 100 million cubic feet flowing down the river past How- Icy Quay. then in the same 24 hours the Canal Company might divert, 10 millions—that is to say, they may re- duce what would have been but for their works 100 Hmillions by 10 per cent. Those are not the words, but that is the only interpretation Jean put upon the section I have no doubt on the evidence that the Canal Com- pany have been exceeding their powers; they have either abstracted or diverted from the river or not allowed to flew therefrom into die canal the water which ought to have been there, and that is a breach of their duty under the Act. I think the preper thing to do is to construe the Act, and then refer the question of the obligations of the cemnesiy to arbitration. If Mr. Warmineton preeses for it I shall grant an injunction, hut shall give such delay as will enable the whole question to be threshed out. Mr. Warmington : Unless we can come to some agree- ment. Immediately before the rising of the Court, counsel tendered to his Lordship in writing the terms they had agreed upon. They were to the effect, that the defendants, their servants, and agents should be restrained until the trial of the action or fur- ther order from abstracting or diverting from the Mersey, or allowing to flow therefrom into the Ship Canal any greater quantity of water than was specified in section 86, sub-section 72, of the Manchester Ship Canal Act. His Lordship said that with one verbal alteration (which counsel made) he thought that would meet the case. He hoped there would not be any difficulty ex- perienced, and he did not anticipate any, but If as sometimes happened difficulty should arise about *is arbitration could he help the parties?

Anothetobjection by Mr. Howcroft is that Socialism would maVe.a man's ability the property of the nation. Does Mr. Hewcroft seriously put this forward as an ob- jection ? If so, it is marvellous. Is this the enli,ghtene; view of an enlightened man in an enlightened age? Is a man endowed with great mental gifts merely for the gratification of selfish ends ? Great Roman generals who pcssessed the gifts indispensable on the field of battle would,when there was no immediate call for their exercise,returned to the plough. This is a general complaint and is not confined , to Socialists ;it is one of the beautiee of the system de- fended by Mr. Howcroft. At all events the two political parties .are finding out that Socialists are very real and that they have votes and know how to use them .—Yours, COMMON jUSTICE.

WARRINGTON COUNTY COWL'.

DISEUTM BETWEEN FARMERS.

At the Warrington C.,unty Court on Thursday, before .flonour Judge IVynne Foulkes, James Brereton, far- mer, Thelvvall, sued Henry Cooke, farmer, Thelwatt, for £3, damage done to a haystack by cattle belonging te the defendant. Mr. A. Browne represented the plaintiff and Mr. H. J. Loegton ths defendant. Cooke also brought in a countsr-claitn for damages to his stack to Vie extract ef in., and £2 10s. for damages to calves by sietentiuu in a field. Toe defendant Cooke had paid 10-s. into court, whish, he contended, was all the daniage done to the plaintiff's stack.—Mr. Browne said that Mr. firereton saw defendant's cattle damaging his stack,and he accordingly warned defendant. He had sent the cattle back many times to the defendant's field.—Mr. Braaten gave evidence in suppsrt of Mr. Browne'd statement, after which in cross-examination he said that the cattle puiled hay out of the stack and damaged it. He had frequently seen the cattle at the stack. The stack was not fenced round except against the road. About 13 yards of stack had been eaten away, being half a cwt. per yard. The whole of the damage had been done by Cooke's cattle. He had not seen other people's cattle at the stack. He sid not fasten up defendant's cattle in a field for a week without food and water.— Evidence was given as to the damage done by several farmers, after which Mr. Longton addressed the Court on behalf of his client. His defence to the claim, he said, was that it was exorbitant. The parties were two far- mers whose land, if it did not actually adjoin in this particular place, adjained in others. There was only one strip of land between, and that was occupied by a farmer named John Whitlow. There was no fence what- ever between the plaintiff's hat d and that of Whitlow's at any rate. Whidow's cattle were unfenced off from the stack, and witnesses bad seen on three different occasions John Whitlow's cattle eating at the stack. There were nine or ten cattle eating at the stack. The sum of 10s. had been paid into court, and that, he con- tended, was the full extent of the damage done. The defendant Cooke was then called, and he denied that the cattle had damaged more than a cwt. of hay. His (defen- ant's) cattle were in a pitiable condition owing to the detention. They had been damaged to the extant of 10s. each animal. He had suffered greater damage than the plaintiff had suffered.—His Honour said that as far as the claim was concerned more might have been claimed than, perhaps, was reasonable, but it was difficult to ray what it was. On the claim he should give a verdict for 503. including what had been paid into court. With regard to the counter-claim he did not se how it could be maintained. The calves were missed a week from the defendant's own prem;sv. and tlspre was no evid- ence to show that they were on plaintiff's land for that time. The plaintiff was not hound to keep his gates open to lot the animals out. If the animals were missing, why did not the defendant make his loss known to the police ? There would, therefore, be judgment for the plaintiff on the counter-claim as well as on the claim. X was a pity the parties had not settled the case without bringing it Into court.

ADVENTURES OF A DIAMOND RING AT WARRINGTON.

A PECULIAR TRANSACTION.

At the Warring!on County Court, on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Floulkes, a case was heard in which Marks Steinart, jeweller, 21, Hey- wood-et., Cheetham Hill-road, Manchester, sued Richard Holcroft, cattle dealer, Appleton Thern.for !.C9 10s., the value of a diamond ring, and .E 2 damages for its detention.-11r. Percy Davies appeared for the plaintiff, and the defendant was represented by Mr. A. Browne.

In opening the case, Mr. Davies said he thought that, if ho was instructed correctly, the defendant had acted most disgracefully throughout. He thought he might go so far as to say that it was questionable whether the case came within the jurisdiction of a civil court at all. However, it was for his Honour to decide this. Theo plaintiff was a very respectable man and very well- known in Warrington, being in the habit of coining to this town once a month to sell jewellery. For some years past he had frequently met the defendant, with whom he had been on friendly terms. On 15th March, 1893.— market day in Warrinetou—the plaintiff, accompanied by a Mr. Parker, called at the Old Coffee House Ho tel, Horsemarket-at. There they saw the defendant Holcroft sitting down. Alter some conversation had taken place between them,Mr. Parker jokingly said to the defendant "Do you want a ring ? "at the same time trying to open the black bag in which the plaintiff carried his jewellery. The bag, however, was locked, and Mr. Parker asked Mr. Steinart for the key, which the latter gave him. Parker then opened the bag, and the defendant held out one of his fingers, upon which Parker placed a diamond ring. All this was done openly, liteinart seeing sverythiag that took place. Holcroft immediately closed his hand and placed it in his pocket. For a few minutes afterwards he stayed in the house, and the plaintiff asked him fir the return of his ring or the payment of its value. The defendant 'replied, "You have nothing to do with it." Steinart, thinking Holcroft was joking, said nothing more about the matter, and the latter then left the house, showing off the diamond ring. On coin- ing to Warrington again a month later, having not in the meantime heard anything from the defendant with reference to the ring, he saw Holcroft, and asked him to return the ring, He replied, " I have net got it with me. Meet me again with Mr. Parker." This satisted the plaintiff. However the defendant did not keep his appointment. On several occasions since that time Mr. Brown, the landlord of the Old Coffee House Hotel, had asked the defendant to give up the ring, as had also the plaintiff and Mr. Parker, and he had promised to do so. On the 27th December last, the plaintiff and defendant again met at the Old Coffee House Hotel. The defendant I held out the finger upon which the ring was sepposed to be. The plaintiff replied, " Chapel," meaning the beautiful place which we possessed in Warrington as a County Court. .(Laughter.) Holcroft then took a bag of gold from his pocket, and tapped Steinart on the face with it, meaning, of course, that he had plenty of money to enable him to fight an action in that court. Mr. Brown said to the defendant on that day," I always thought, Mr. Holcroft, you were a gentleman, or von would have squared the matter up before now." Hl- croft replied, "I always have been a gentleman, but Steinart will never get his ring back while his heart is warm." This was a most extraordinary expression to make use of. That morning, before the case was called on, the defendant expressed his willingness to re- turn the ring, if the plaintiff would pay Mr. Browne's costs.

The plaintiff was then called, and corroborated this statement.

In stating the case for the defendant,Mr. Browne said the conduct of Holcroft had been characterised as dis- graceful. He thought, hovrever,that it was Parker who had acted in a disgraceful manner. The most extraor- dinary fact about the matter was that Parker had carried Steinart's bag from his (Parker's) house in Fennel-st.,to the Old Coffee House Hotel, showing that Steinart had absolute confidence in him. Not only this, but he actu- ally gave Parker the key with which to open the bag. In short, he was a party to the whole transaction. He was entirely as nsuch to blame as Parker if the matter was a joke, but the defendant denied that it was a joke. He had been made a present of the ring. The defendant then gave evidence, and having de- tailed how the ring was placed on his finger added that Steinart said ba him, " Dick, you're all right. I won't ask you for a penny. I'll make Charlie Parker pay R 9 10s. for the rine." It was not true that Steinart asked for the return of the ring. He did not ask either for the ring or the money. After defendant's evidence the court rose. The Case therefore stands adjourned until next court day.

MR. C. FENIVICK M.P., ON THE LABOUR QUESTION.

ADDRESS AT LYMM.

On Monday evening, Mr. C. Fenwick, M.P., gave an address on the" Labour Question," in the Wes- leyan schoolroom at Lymm,the chair being occupied by Mr. J. Dodds. Mr. Funwiek said that tits people of this country were to be congratulated upon the feet that there was in office one of the most democratic governments this country had ever seen, and that there was in Parliament a. larger number of men directly coming from the labour- ing classes than had keen known in any former period. i The question was often asked—What would be the ulti- mate result of all the present social and political ferment and agitation ? Any answer to that must be a matter of conjecture ; tut he was quite res.ly ta help on the cause of progress and leave the future to the discretion and general sense of right and justice which pervaded 1 the minds of the great majority of the people.—(Heer, hear.)—The labour movement, as he understood it, was a social and political movement, originating with the industrial portion of the community, and representing a very natural, reasonable and legitimate desire on their part to seeure for themselves and their families more healthy and comfortable environment. The classes which in past years had hed political power had abused their trust, and no one need, therefore, be surprised at I seeing to-day this movement, in which the great body of the working classes were banded, and in which they meant to make a successful fight. Some people feared that the labouring people would in their turn practice the same selfishness and injustices as other classes bad practised before them. The only policy, however, for the working classes, was justice, equality between man and man, and the downthrow of all class distinctions. (Applause.) Be recognised that a great responsibility rested on those es ho stood at the head of this movement. It behoved every man in a pesition of influence to weigh well his words and to do nothing which in his quiet mementos would cause him regret to reflect upon. Here and there they found, he knew, an excess of violent lan- guage, and here and there unreasonable proposals.. He would advise them not to take alarm, however, at these things. So long as there were free speech and tree criticism he had no fear as to the future of the country. It was contended by some that the labour programme could only be carried into effect by the formation of a separate and independent labour party. Those wile ad- vocated such a course were, he 'relieved, mistaken in the policy they adopted. Heaven forbid that they should over fall into the serious mistake—as he regarded it—of which they saw examples on the Contineut—namely, the multiplication, almost without limit, of political sects and parties. Nothing would give a greater check to the cause which they honestly advocated than that men should be largely divided in that way. There was only room in this country for two political parties, aid he believed that the working classes would best con- sult their own interests by allying themselves with one or other of these parties. They were both provided with political machinery and experience, and why should the working classes dispossess themselves of the benefit of these when they were honestly invited to make use of them? The moment the working classes did ignore existing parties and organisations they ran the risk of the two political parties forming a coalition to thwart them in the objects they had in view. His advice to working- men was to ally themselves with the Liberal party. (Applause.) The Liberal party was the party of progress and-reform, and its leaders wore men whose traditions and life bespoke sympathy and support. It was con- tended by some people, toe, that the only remedy for evils which existed to-day was that the State ahould take over the management and control of all methods and implements of production, and that capital, of every shape and form, should be nationa- lised. hut that was rather a large order. The history of the dockyards and the post office showed that Wages and conditions of labour under the Govern- ment control were too often not equal to those found in the establishments of private employers. He looked with great suspicion upon the State being made in any sense the manager and controller of all Industries. They could manage their own affairs infinitely better, more economically, and with better results to the industrial elastics by individual enterprise. In the settlement of industrial disputes that occurred in the future there would have to be give and take ou both sides. Some men ignored and decried arbitration and conciliation. Hut all history, and especially all industrial history, taught them that. rea-on and judgment must ultimately determine the issue et stake ; and why not, therefore, bring in those considerations at the commencement of the conflict, and so prevent open hositilities and the misery which industrial war brought in its train ? Not- withstanding the great crisis which from time to time arose, he firmly believed that they were making pro- geese, and that better days were ahead. (Applause.)

THE WARRINUTON SWINIMING:CLUB.

IZEPORT OF THE COMMITTEE.

The Warrington Swimming Club have had a very ..uicessful season, especitaly when it is taken into consideration that it is their first. According to a report which will be submitted next week to the Committee, by the lion. treas. (Mr.II. Toft), there is a balance in hand of several pounds. The report of the committee drawn up by the secretaries, Messrs Stanley Wilson (deputy town clerk), and Mr. T. G. Grounds, states :—We have great pleasure hi submit- ting our report to you on the past reason's work. Con- , sidering that the club was formed so late in the sensor' we think the club is to be congratulated upon the success achieved—both as regards its financial position and the large roll of membership. It will be remembered that a public meeting was held at the Town Hall in June 1803, and the following resolution passed " That steps be taken forthwith to form a Swimming club in conneetio* with the baths." At this meeting about 25 names were given in, and a committee anti officers ap- pointed. The roll of membership rapidly ineressed,ancl at the present time the club has a roll of over 70 mem- bers, The work of organisation was a somewhat ddlicult task. An excellent code of rules was drawn up And the necessary appliances for polo procured. This necessitated an expenditure which will not again be required and your committee have every reason to be- lieve that the season ot 1894- will be even more profitable than the past. During the short season two galas were held and were in every respect most suceessfui. Your club was honoured by the semi anal of the Northern Counties Champion Water PAD Competition being played in their baths, but this was a souree of consider- able expense. In addition to paying the Association £lO we were compelled to give prizes of greater value than would have been the case if the gala had been entirely ander our own Committee'n eoetrol. Your Committee have noticed with satisfaction the progress made in swimming by the members of the club—especially the . younger members—and the thanks of the club are due to the many members who have encouraged and assisted , those wanting advice. The snip-committee appointed Ito award certificates for efficiency in swimming have held two trials as a result °fly:Olds 6 members secured the second class certificate and bronze medal,and 6 who gained the third class. No first class certificates were gained. In conclusion your committee beg to make the following recommendations :—(1) That in order to en- lighten the work of the general secretary a gala sub- committee consisting of four members of the general committee and hon, secretary be appointed. (2) That the said committee make and carry out all a-range- meats for the granting of certificates."

WEEKLY REPORT ON THE HEALTH ( WARRINGTON.

During the week, which was the Bth of the current year, the following births and deaths were registered :—Births : Warrington, 50 ; Latchford, 3 ; t0ta1,53. Deaths: Warrington. 15; Latchford, 1; total 16. The excess of births over deaths was 37. The death-rate from all causes was equal to 14.5 per 1,000, against 19-9 the average of the previous four weeks, and against 17.9 the corresponding week of last year. The death-rate from the seven chief zymotic diseases was equal to 2.8 per 1,000, against , 2.0 the average rate of the four previous weeks, and: against 3.7 the corresponding week of 1893. The ages of mortality were :-0 to 1,3; 1 to 5, 5 ; 5 to 15,1 ; 15 to 25, 0 ; 25 to GO, 4; 60 up 3: total, 16. The datlis included-2 from measles; , 1 front whooping clugh ; 1 from influenza; 3 from tubercular disease; 3 from old age; 1 from lung disease ; 1 from liver disease ; 1 from brain disease. METEOROLOGY.—The mean temperature was 35'5 Fah., and therefore 51 deg. lower than the preced- ing week. The maximum temperature 48 F., was recorded on the 24th inst., and -the minimum temperature 25 F., on the 19th inst. The rainfad ineaszsred 18 inch. The general direction was easterly, the reading of the Anemometer denoting 510 ritiles.—JonN H. GORNALL, Medical Officer of Health.

THE SHIP CANAL.

MEETING OF SHAREHOLDERS. THE QUEEN TO OPEN THE CANAL

Mr. J. K. Bytholl presided on Wednesday at the seven- teenth ordinary general meeting of the shareholders of the Ship Canal Company. In the course of his address he made the interesting announcement that Her Majt sty the Queen intends, if she is able to do so, to perform the oeremony of opening the canal in the summer. Mr. By- then said Manchester was now one of the great seaports of the world. Ocean geing ships of large size bad al- ready conic to Manchester,and sailed thence to the roost distant ports. When unofficially opened on the Ist of January the canal was admittedly in an incomplete state. Bearing that fact in wind, ha thought the traffic already secured was in a high degree encouraging. An enormous improvement of the waterway had been effected in the course of the last two manths,and dredlf- ing was still activaly in progress. In a very short time they might expect it to be ready for any vessel of less than 261 t. draught. As to finances, he was glad t,o say that after all the claims of contractors had been met they would have a surplus available to spend on future works of £OO,OOO. In regard to return cargoes for ships which came to Manchester he could report a steady im- provement, and there was no question but that their brightest hopes would be realised in course of time. Farrington as an export centre for coal had already e.. ta Wished a successful trade., One single contract entered into was for 60,000 tons of South Yorkshire coal, to be delivered within the next six months. At Runcorn and Saltport also there was good "back-loading." Mr. By- thell laid stress on the fact that the Canal Company were not carriers, and that it was necessary for shipper, to make their own arrangements both for the placing of cargoes en shipboard and for their distribution. The company left these matters, as also the establishment of receiving warehouses, to private enterprise. After some questions had been put and anewereti by the Chairman, the half-yearly report was adopted amid applause. A proposal to reduce the fees of the directors from L 4,400 to £3,500 was made by the directors themselves,and wa: rejected almost unanimously.

LYMM.

SAD DEATH OF A POSTMAN.—Much regret has been felt in the village at the sad death of Mr. James Holt, Wal- ton House, Lyinm, which occurred on Saturday night. The deceased, who was employed as a postman, was about 23 years of age. On Monday week he complained of illness, and consulted a doctor, who ordered him to bed. Towards the end of the week deceased grew some- what worse, and on Friday night left the house. Ile was found at Statham and taken home, but remained in a critical condition until the next evening, when he died. He was a member of the Lymm Volunteer eorps. The funeral took place on Tuesday afternoon, when, in. addition to a number of postmen, the Volunteer hand and company followed the hearse to the Parish Church- yard, where the deceased was buried. The hand played the " Dead March," and a firing party shot three volleys over the grave.

RAGGED SUNDAY SCHOOL.

Mr. W. Sparks, financial secretary of the Warring- ton Ragged Sunday School, esires to acknowledge the receipt of .E 6 108.4 d., being the amount realized rom Mr.W.Browa's concert, held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, on Tuesday, February 13th. He also wishes to thank Mr. Brown for his valuable help, as well as the ladies and gentlemen wjlo gave their services on that occasion. WORKINGMEN'S GOSPEL TEMPERANCE MISSION.

On Sunday the fourth anniversary of the above was celebrated.A service at 2.30 was held in the af ter- noon,when Idr.E J. Guest, J.P..presided,and gave an earnest address. In the evening there was a large audience. Mr. Taylor. P.C.M.,presided,and the Rev.B. , Percival,vicar of St.Peter's,spoke upon the words in ' Corinthians 1.x,23. The speaker remarked that while the moderate use of alcoholic drinks might be bene- ficial, yet in view of the awful amount of poverty and misery caused by drink in the present day, the best and wisest course was total abstinence. On Monday evening a social tea was held at 6.30, and at 7.30 a public meeting, at which Miss Dutton, of Moore, presided, and gave a very earnest address. An encouraging report was read by Mr. llorrocks, after which the meeting was addressed by Miss Green, Mr. Firth (Manchester), Mr. Taylor, P.C.M., and Mr. Thornton. During the evening a hand- somely engraved water jug was presented to Mr. Firth, for hi a zeal and help during the year. The jug bore a suitable inscription, and was the work of Mr. Rigby. Misses Egginton and Sutton gave a duet on the piano, and Mr. Sam Stringer a solo, which were well received. A hearty vote of thanks was given to all who had taken part in the proceed- ings.—On Tuesday evening the Band of Hope be- longing to this Mission held their quarterly tea meeting, when about 60 sat down to an excellent feast. Afterwards a very enjoyable evening we., "spent.." Mr. Down's magic lantern view was heartily enjoyed by the children. A competition for prizes, in songs, and recitals, was keenly en- tered into by about 15 members of the band, and many of the pieces were given in a creditable man- ner. The songs of Miss Peers and Mary Cruik- shanks were received with applause by the parents and friends. Prizes useful and ornamental, were then given to the winners by Messrs. Ilorrocka and Rigby.

BOLD TEA PARTY., The annual tea party and concert in aid of tho Bold Schools took place in the schools on Thurs- day evening last. The room had been tastefully decorated with evergreens and banners by Mrs. Jas. Garton, Miss Garton and Miss Almond, and an excellent tea was supplied by Mrs. Gibbs (Sutton), to which some 180 persons did ample justice. ! Amongst those preent were the Rev. Wright- Williams and Mrs. Wright-Williams, Rev. J. Tv Evans, Mesdames Chadwick, Newton, C. Whitfield, Critchley, Mr. and Mrs. Young (Bold Hain, Mr. and Mrs. A. Johnson, Mr., Mrs., Miss and Miss 'l'. Baker, ! Mr., Mrs. and Miss Garton (May Pole), Mr. and Mrs. John Garton, Misses Johnson, L. Wsbster, Eatnek, E. Eatock, Lowe, Newiek ; Messrs. Johnson (Old Hall), T. Garton, F. Eatock, J. E. Johns m,Websier, J. Murray, Armour, Morris (Sr. delers), and others. After the groaning boards had been relieved of their • dainties a musical programme was gone through. The Rev. Wright-Williams occupied the chair and said, that he had to welcome all present to their school that night. Their school was a voluntary one, and they had up to the present the pleasure oi being without a School Board, which meant economy to the ratepayers and full advance in • education. It was the wisn of the Bold ratepayers to carry on their school in the voluntary way and they would like to have the number of Slubscri hers

incr-eased. The programme was then proceeded with. Miss M. A. Robinson executed in admirable style two pisnoforte solos, Miss Ashton sang in capital voice " Love's old sweet song," and being encored " The deep blue wave." Mr. F. W. Bayliss followed with "They all love Jack," encore" Land of the brave "; and later on in the evening he sang " Tile wolf." Mr. Wilson Blackledge gave his hum- orous sketches " Our street at 8 a.m.," " Village ' choir concert," "The baby," " Once upon a time," for all of which he was loudly applauded and gave as encores "Imitations of mandoline awl banjo," and "The market ground." Mrs. Young sang with deep feeling "Rory Darlin," and being encored played the hones, later she sari, "A daisy song,' encore "Tit for tat." Mr. C. Ilelsby greatly amused the audience with his songs "Sold every- where" and " It's courtin' neet," to which he gave as an encore, "I've enough and to spare." Misses A. Ashton and J. Murray, sang "The sea of glass " very creditably, and Miss J. Murray, notwithstand- ing a severe cold from which she was suffering, •.gave a beautiful rendering of "Killarney," for which she was loudly and deservedly applauded. The Rev. J. T. Evans very ably accompanied. Mr. Young proposed, and Mr. Webster sdconded, a vote of thanks to the subscribers, artistes, workers and the Vicar for presiding. Great credit is due to Mrs. and Miss Garton (May Pole), for organising the entertainment which all admit was one of the most successful ever held in Bold, and promises a large addition to the school fund. The evening's enter- tainmant was brought to a close in the usual loyal manner.
